# AP-15 — Extra Points (EX) — Kinh Ngoại Kỳ Huyệt (经外奇穴)

> **Status:** Phase 2 COMPLETE — 47/47 entries + REVIEW DONE (2026-08-19).
> This file contains 47 entries: EX-HN1-15 (15 entries, EX-HN COMPLETE),
> EX-B1-B8 (8 entries, EX-B COMPLETE), EX-CA1 (1 entry, EX-CA COMPLETE),
> EX-UE1-UE11 (11 entries, EX-UE COMPLETE), EX-LE1-LE12 (12 entries,
> EX-LE COMPLETE).
> **ALL 5 REGIONS COMPLETE.** **13/13 Ghost Points COMPLETE.**
> **REVIEW COMPLETE:** 47/47 Bằng chứng sections, heading consistency,
> count reconciled (47 WHO EX codes, not 48). 2 ⚡ framework notes
> (both EX-HN3). 3A + 18B + 26C.
>
> **Category overview:** Extra points (经外奇穴, jīng wài qí xué,
> literally "extraordinary points outside the channels") are acupoints
> NOT located on any of the 14 main meridians but recognized for clinical
> effectiveness. WHO-WPRO (Western Pacific Regional Office) Part 2
> (1988) standardized 48 extra points.
> Unlike meridian points which follow channel pathways, extra points are
> organized by anatomical region: EX-HN (head/neck), EX-CA
> (chest/abdomen), EX-B (back), EX-UE (upper extremity), EX-LE (lower
> extremity). Extra points have no Five Shu classification, no
> Yuan-Source/Luo-Connecting/Xi-Cleft relationships, and no Eight
> Confluent pairings. Their therapeutic value derives from specific
> anatomical location rather than channel theory.
> → Cross-ref: B01 §1.3.3 (extra point definition)
>
> **Total points:** 48 WHO standard | Entries completed: 47/48

## EX-HN — Head & Neck (头颈部)

### EX-HN3 — Ấn Đường (印堂, Yìntáng)

**Evidence tier: A (Deep Analysis)**

The "Hall of Impression" — most clinically used extra point in modern
acupuncture practice. 2nd most-used acupoint in depression neuroimaging
studies (10/31 studies, after GV20). Located between the eyebrows in
trigeminal V1 territory — the same nerve territory targeted by
FDA-approved external trigeminal nerve stimulation (eTNS) devices.
Cross-tradition convergence with Sthapani Marma (Ayurveda) and Ajna
Chakra association.

#### Vị trí giải phẫu

WHO standard location (WPRO 2008): on the forehead, at the midpoint
between the medial ends of the two eyebrows.

Anatomical landmarks: at the glabella, the smooth eminence on the
frontal bone between the superciliary arches. In practice, found by
locating the midpoint between the medial extremities of the eyebrows
on the frontal midline.

Needle depth range: 3-8 mm (0.3-0.5 cun) transverse/oblique insertion,
directed inferiorly toward the nose bridge or superiorly toward GV24.
Pinching technique recommended — lift the skin between thumb and index
finger before inserting to control depth and prevent slipping.
Perpendicular deep insertion is NOT standard: frontal bone lies directly
beneath the thin muscle layers.

#### Cấu trúc mô học tại điểm

Nerve supply: supratrochlear nerve (from the frontal nerve, a branch of
the ophthalmic division V1 of trigeminal nerve cranial nerve V). The
supraorbital nerve (also V1, exiting through the supraorbital foramen
or notch approximately 2.5 cm lateral) provides adjacent innervation.
Both nerves are sensory branches of V1 carrying Aβ (touch), Aδ
(fast pain), and C fiber (slow pain) information.

Motor innervation: temporal branches of facial nerve (cranial nerve VII)
supply the procerus and corrugator supercilii muscles at this location.

Spinal segment: NONE — cranial nerve territory. This is a fundamental
distinction from all limb and trunk acupoints: V1 afferents project
DIRECTLY to the trigeminal sensory nuclear complex in the brainstem
WITHOUT spinal cord relay.
→ Cross-ref: A07 §1.7 (facial somatotopy), A01 §1.4 (cranial nerve V)

Fascial plane: superficial fascia of the forehead. Muscle layers from
superficial to deep: skin → subcutaneous tissue → procerus muscle
(depresses medial brow, creates horizontal nasal wrinkles) →
corrugator supercilii muscle (draws brow medially, creates vertical
frown lines) → periosteum of frontal bone.

Vascular: supratrochlear artery and vein (from ophthalmic artery, a
branch of internal carotid). Branches of supraorbital artery adjacent.
Superficial vessels — bruising is common at this location.
→ Cross-ref: A01, A03, A07

CT afferents: PRESENT. Forehead skin is hairy (non-glabrous) → CT
(C-tactile) afferents are present, unlike glabrous skin (palms, soles).
This means gentle touch at Yintang can activate the CT → posterior
insula → affective touch pathway in addition to the trigeminal-vagal
pathway. Dual pathway access (trigeminal-vagal + CT afferent) may
contribute to the calming/anxiolytic effects.
→ Cross-ref: C01 §4.7 (CT afferent distribution), A06 §3.5

#### Phân loại huyệt (Point Category Membership)

Extra point (经外奇穴): Yintang is classified as EX-HN3 (Extra
Head/Neck point #3) in the WHO standard nomenclature. Not located on
any of the 14 main meridians. WHO-WPRO Part 2 (1988) codified this
point based on clinical effectiveness despite absence from the channel
system.

Five Shu: not applicable (extra point, not a meridian point)
Yuan-Source: not applicable
Luo-Connecting: not applicable
Xi-Cleft: not applicable
Back-Shu: not applicable
Front-Mu: not applicable
Eight Confluent: not applicable
Hui-Meeting: not applicable

Special designations:
- Arguably the MOST clinically used non-meridian point in modern
  acupuncture and acupressure practice
- Part of standard neuropsychiatric protocol: GV20 + Yintang + HT7
  ± Four Gates (LI4+LR3) — this combination appears across clinical
  guidelines, textbooks, and research protocols
- Adjacent point for eye problems (B01 §3.2): proximity to orbit
- NOT Ma Dan-yang Star Point (meridian points only)
- NOT Sun Si-miao Ghost Point
→ Cross-ref: B01 §1.3.3 (extra point classification)

#### Cross-tradition mapping

**1. TCM acupoint:** EX-HN3 (Yintang, 印堂, "Hall of Impression" or
"Seal Hall"). 印 = seal/stamp/impression, 堂 = hall/court. Traditional
functions: calm the spirit (安神), clear the head and brighten the eyes
(清头目), benefit the nose (利鼻). Primary indications: frontal headache,
dizziness, rhinitis, sinusitis, epistaxis, eye diseases, insomnia,
anxiety, infantile convulsion. In modern practice, the most-used
non-channel point for neuropsychiatric conditions.

**2. Trigger point:** Procerus muscle trigger point — referred pain to
the bridge of the nose and area between the eyes. Corrugator supercilii
trigger point — referred pain to the medial forehead above the eyebrow.
Both muscles lie directly at the Yintang location. Dorsher's systematic
mapping identifies trigger point overlap at this site. Palpation of
procerus/corrugator tension is a common finding in tension-type
headache and stress-related facial muscle contraction.

**3. Ashi correspondence:** The glabella is a common site for
frontal headache complaints. Sinus pain/pressure is frequently
localized between the eyebrows. Palpation tenderness at this location
is a standard clinical finding in tension-type headache, frontal
sinusitis, and stress-related presentations. Patients spontaneously
identify "between the eyebrows" as a site of tension and discomfort.

**4. Marma point:** Sthapani (स्थपनी). One of 107 Marma points in
Ayurveda. Classification: Sira Marma (vascular type — reflects the
superficial supratrochlear vessels). Location: between the eyebrows,
at the center of the forehead. Size: ½ anguli (approximately 1 cm).
Injury classification: Sadyah Pranahara (immediately lethal if severely
injured — historical classification reflecting the proximity to
intracranial venous sinuses). Associated with Ajna Chakra (6th chakra,
"Third Eye"). Therapeutic functions: controls mind and senses, relieves
headaches, anxiety, stress, insomnia, enhances concentration and mental
clarity. Marma Chikitsa (Ayurvedic point therapy) at Sthapani uses
gentle pressure for calming and grounding.

CROSS-TRADITION CONVERGENCE: TCM Yintang and Ayurvedic Sthapani occupy
the SAME anatomical location (midpoint between eyebrows) and share
nearly identical therapeutic indications (calming, mental clarity,
headache, insomnia, anxiety). Two independent medical traditions
arriving at the same point for the same purposes — both now explicable
by the shared V1 trigeminal-vagal pathway to brainstem parasympathetic
centers.

**5. Head zone:** V1 ophthalmic dermatome. NO viscerotome projection
— the face does NOT project to specific internal organs via
dermatome-viscerotome convergence (Beissner 2011). The forehead
dermatome (V1) has no organ-specific segmental relationship. This is
the same finding as GV20: therapeutic effects on mood, sleep, and
autonomic function cannot be explained by Head zone segmental
convergence — consistent with a supraspinal trigeminal-brainstem
mechanism.
→ Cross-ref: AP-13 (GV20 — same finding)

**6. Anatomy Trains:** Superficial Front Line (SFL) reaches the
forehead via sternocleidomastoid (SCM) → scalp fascia. The frontalis
muscle connects to the galea aponeurotica, which is the cranial
terminus of both the SFL (anterior) and the Superficial Back Line (SBL,
posterior). However, Yintang sits at the procerus/corrugator level
(facial expression muscles), which are not primary components of
Myers' myofascial meridian system. Limited Anatomy Trains significance
compared to GV20 (SBL/SFL terminus) or KI1 (SBL origin at plantar
fascia).
→ Cross-ref: A03 §3.3

**7. Neurogenic spot:** No data. Kim 2017's neurogenic inflammatory
spot mapping focused on trunk dermatomes (T1-L2). Facial points were
not included in the study protocol.

**8. Cupping point:** Not applicable. The glabella is a small, bony,
concave area between the superciliary arches — anatomically unsuitable
for cupping (no flat surface for cup adhesion, proximity to eyes).

**Convergence score: 4/8** (TCM, Trigger Point, Ashi, Marma).
Moderate-high convergence for a facial point. The Sthapani Marma
correspondence is the strongest cross-tradition finding: same location,
same indications, independent traditions. Lower than back/limb points
(which access Head zones, cupping, neurogenic spots) because facial
anatomy precludes cupping and Kim 2017 did not study cranial regions.

#### Chỉ định truyền thống (DATA — ghi nhận, không endorse as mechanism)

Nguồn cổ:
- Zhenjiu Dacheng (针灸大成, Yang Jizhou, 1601): "Impression Hall" —
  calms the spirit, clears the head, benefits the nose. Treats frontal
  headache, dizziness, rhinitis, epistaxis, infantile convulsion.
  Noted as a point "outside the channels" (经外) with proven clinical
  efficacy.
- Extra point collections in various classical texts identify Yintang
  for spirit-calming and nasal conditions. The point appears in
  supplementary chapters of major acupuncture texts rather than the
  main channel descriptions.

Nguồn hiện đại:
- Deadman (Manual of Acupuncture): calms the spirit, treats frontal
  headache, dizziness, rhinitis, sinusitis, epistaxis, eye pain,
  insomnia, infantile convulsion.
- WHO (2008): headache (frontal), dizziness, rhinitis, insomnia.
- Chinese clinical guidelines: Yintang included in standard depression
  and insomnia acupuncture protocols.

Kinh nghiệm lâm sàng nổi bật:
- **2nd most-used point in depression neuroimaging studies** (10/31
  studies, after GV20 at 13) — systematic review of magnetic resonance
  imaging studies on acupuncture for depression (Zhong et al. 2021,
  Frontiers in Human Neuroscience, PMC8417590). This represents
  remarkable clinical consensus for a non-meridian point.
- **Standard combination for neuropsychiatric conditions:** GV20 +
  Yintang + HT7 (Shenmen) ± Four Gates (LI4+LR3). This protocol
  appears across clinical guidelines, research protocols, and textbook
  recommendations — consistent across independent sources.
- **Standard insomnia protocol:** GV20 + Yintang + HT7 + SP6 (Chinese
  Medicine guideline recommendation).
- **Adjacent point for eye problems** (B01 §3.2): used for various
  ophthalmic conditions due to proximity to orbit and V1 innervation.
- **Most-used non-meridian point** in modern acupuncture practice,
  to the degree that some practitioners treat it functionally as if
  it were a GV (Governing Vessel) point lying on the anterior midline
  between GV24 (Shenting) and GV25 (Suliao).
→ Cross-ref: AP-13 (GV20+Yintang standard combination), AP-05
(HT7+GV20+Yintang anxiety protocol)

#### Bằng chứng hiện đại

**① Preoperative anxiety — 🟡 Plausible**

Kwon & Lee 2018 (Medical Acupuncture, PMC5908420): preliminary review
of 5 randomized controlled trials using acupuncture or acupressure at
Yintang (EX-HN3) ALONE for anxiety. All 5 trials targeted preoperative
anxiety. Consistent finding: significant reduction in self-reported
anxiety (State-Trait Anxiety Inventory, STAI). The reduction was
specific to state anxiety (STAI-S), not trait anxiety (STAI-T) —
consistent with an acute anxiolytic effect rather than personality
change.

Acar et al. 2013 (Journal of Alternative and Complementary Medicine):
randomized controlled trial, N=52 adult surgical patients.
Single-point, 20-minute acupuncture at Yintang vs sham point. STAI-S
decreased significantly in the acupuncture group (p=0.018); no change
in sham group. Bispectral index (BIS) also showed sedation trend.

Limitations: all trials in preoperative setting (high-anxiety
population, short-term intervention); small sample sizes; limited sham
comparisons; methodological quality concerns noted by Kwon & Lee. The
consistency of direction across 5 independent trials is notable, but
more rigorous trials needed.

The acupressure anxiety meta-analysis (Chen SR et al. 2023 — 24
randomized controlled trials, 2,537 participants, State-trait anxiety
inventory reduction standardized mean difference −1.30) used HT7 + PC6
+ EX-HN3 (Yintang) as the most common point combination. Individual
Yintang contribution not isolated.
→ Cross-ref: AP-05 (Chen SR 2023 in HT7 entry)

**② Depression — 🟡 Plausible**

Yintang is the 2nd most-used acupoint in depression neuroimaging
research (10/31 functional magnetic resonance imaging studies, after
GV20). Always used in multi-point protocols — no sole-point depression
randomized controlled trial exists.

Zheng et al. 2012 (Acupuncture in Medicine): functional magnetic
resonance imaging study of electroacupuncture at GV20 + Yintang in 12
healthy volunteers. 30-minute stimulation followed by resting-state
scans at 5 and 15 minutes post-stimulation. Regional homogeneity (ReHo)
changes observed in temporal lobe, cerebellum, fusiform gyrus, and
limbic structures at different timepoints — brain regions associated
with emotion processing and default mode network.

Limitations: GV20+Yintang stimulated simultaneously (individual
contribution not separable); healthy volunteers, not depressed patients;
small N. Neuroimaging shows brain activation changes but does not
establish clinical efficacy for depression.

**③ Insomnia — 🟡 Plausible**

Part of the standard insomnia acupuncture protocol: GV20 + Yintang +
HT7 + SP6.

Liu et al. 2021 (Annals of Translational Medicine): randomized
controlled trial, N=60, GV20+Yintang+HT7+SP6 vs sham acupuncture,
3×/week for 4 weeks. Acupuncture group showed significantly improved
Pittsburgh Sleep Quality Index (PSQI), sleep efficiency, and reduced
anxiety/depression scores.

Limitations: multi-point protocol (Yintang contribution not isolated);
N=60; single trial. The consistent appearance of Yintang in insomnia
protocols across independent research groups suggests clinical
consensus, but this may reflect tradition rather than evidence.
→ Cross-ref: AP-13 (Liu 2021 in GV20 entry)

**④ Frontal headache / rhinitis — 🔴 Unvalidated**

Anatomical rationale is strong: V1 territory innervates the forehead,
and frontal headache is mediated through V1 afferents. Adjacent to
paranasal sinuses. But no sole-point controlled clinical trial for
headache or rhinitis at Yintang. Traditional indication supported by
anatomical logic and clinical experience but lacking controlled
evidence.

#### Dynamic acupoint properties

Limited data. Yintang is on the forehead (relatively uniform tissue
regardless of pathology), and no disease-state sensitization studies
have been conducted specifically at this point. Clinical observation
suggests increased tenderness at Yintang in tension-type headache and
during stress states, but no controlled measurement data.

Supraorbital notch vs foramen anatomical variant (present in
approximately 50% of individuals per side) may affect nerve
accessibility and stimulation response, but this has not been studied
in the acupuncture context.
→ Cross-ref: C04 §4 (Dynamic Acupoint Phenomenon)

#### Cơ chế giải thích (BSA framework attribution)

**Neural pathway — trigeminal-vagal coupling (A02 §4.5):**

```
Step 1: Needle/pressure activates V1 supratrochlear nerve afferents
        at the glabella
Step 2: V1 afferents → trigeminal ganglion → trigeminal sensory
        nuclear complex (brainstem — DIRECT, no spinal cord relay)
Step 3: Trigeminal nucleus connects to:
        → NTS (nucleus tractus solitarius) → autonomic integration
        → DMN (dorsal motor nucleus of vagus) → parasympathetic
        → LC (locus coeruleus) → norepinephrine modulation
        → DRN (dorsal raphe nucleus) → serotonin modulation
        → Reticular formation → arousal/sedation
Step 4: Downstream effects:
        → Vagal activation → ↓heart rate, ↓blood pressure
        → Norepinephrine/serotonin modulation → anxiolysis, mood regulation
        → Arousal modulation → sedation, sleep facilitation
```

**Comparison with GV20 mechanism:**

Both GV20 and Yintang access the trigeminal-brainstem pathway, but with
a key difference:
- GV20 = DUAL innervation (V1 supraorbital + C2 greater occipital).
  Two afferent pathways converge on the same brainstem targets.
  Identified molecular effector: paraventricular nucleus (PVN) oxytocin.
- Yintang = PURE V1 innervation (supratrochlear nerve only). Single
  cranial nerve pathway. No identified molecular effector.

This makes Yintang a "purer" V1 test point than GV20. If Yintang
produces similar neuropsychiatric effects to GV20 through V1 alone
(without C2 contribution), this would confirm that the V1
trigeminal-vagal pathway is SUFFICIENT for the anxiolytic/calming
effects — the C2 component at GV20 adds to but is not required for
the basic effect.

**Segmental analysis: NO segmental gap.**

Unlike limb/trunk points that must bridge non-segmental gaps to reach
their therapeutic targets (for example ST36 L4-S1 → abdominal organs
T5-T12 = 5+ segment gap; KI1 L4-S2 → kidney T10-L1 = 6 segment gap),
Yintang's effects on anxiety, mood, and sleep are mediated through
DIRECT brainstem access. The V1 trigeminal pathway reaches NTS, DMN of
vagus, LC, and DRN directly — no segmental relay, no convergence zone
needed. The targets (brainstem autonomic centers) are "on-path" for the
trigeminal afferents.

| Phase 1 point | Pathway | Segmental gap | Molecular effector |
|---|---|---|---|
| ST36 | PROKR2 → vagal-adrenal | 5+ segments | PROKR2 🟢 |
| PC6 | Median nerve → NTS | 6 segments | gamma-aminobutyric acid (NTS) 🟡 |
| LI4 | Radial → periaqueductal gray (PAG)/rostroventromedial medulla (RVM) | 4 segments | PAG enkephalin 🟡 |
| SP6 | Dual S2-S4 + PAG | 3 segments | Dual pathway 🟡 |
| BL67 | UNKNOWN | UNKNOWN | UNKNOWN 🔴 |
| Back-Shu | Somatovisceral reflex | 0 (segmental) | Segmental 🟢 |
| HT7 | Ulnar → cuneate → limbic | 5 segments | Cuneate-limbic 🟡 |
| LR3 | Deep peroneal → thalamus | 5 segments | Cortical 🟡 |
| GV20 | V1+C2 → brainstem → PVN | 0 (direct) | Oxytocin 🟢 |
| KI1 | Intensity-dependent | 6-9 segments | Non-specific 🔴 |
| **Yintang** | **V1 → brainstem direct** | **0 (direct)** | **Unknown** |

Yintang and GV20 share the distinction of having NO segmental gap —
both access brainstem targets directly through cranial nerve pathways.
This contrasts sharply with limb points that must rely on convergence
zones or ascending tract mechanisms to reach non-segmental targets.

**eTNS convergence — FDA-validated neural pathway:**

The external trigeminal nerve stimulation (eTNS) device (Cefaly)
transcutaneously stimulates the supratrochlear and supraorbital
branches of V1 at the forehead — EXACTLY the same nerve territory as
Yintang. Cefaly is FDA-cleared for migraine prevention and acute
treatment. The Monarch eTNS device (same V1 territory) received
FDA approval in 2019 for attention deficit hyperactivity disorder
treatment in pediatric patients.

This creates a unique situation: the NEURAL PATHWAY that Yintang
accesses has been validated by FDA-approved devices, even though the
DELIVERY METHOD differs (needle/pressure vs transcutaneous electrical
stimulation). The eTNS evidence establishes that V1 stimulation at the
forehead CAN produce clinically significant neurological effects. The
key question remains: does mechanical stimulation (acupuncture at
3-8 mm depth, or acupressure at the surface) activate V1 afferents at
sufficient intensity to replicate eTNS's brainstem effects?

The Acute Migraine Therapy with External Trigeminal Neurostimulation
study: eTNS produced 59% migraine pain intensity reduction at 1 hour vs
30% sham (device at same location but sub-threshold stimulation).
→ Cross-ref: C04 §3.5.1 (face = highest-privilege brainstem access)

**CT afferent dual pathway:**

Unlike GV20 (which is on the scalp where CT density may be lower) and
unlike KI1 (glabrous sole, NO CT afferents), forehead skin has both:
(a) CT afferents for affective touch → posterior insula → PVN →
oxytocin pathway, AND (b) V1 trigeminal → brainstem direct pathway.
Acupressure at Yintang may activate BOTH pathways simultaneously: CT
for calming/soothing and trigeminal-vagal for parasympathetic
activation. This dual access is possible ONLY because the forehead is
hairy skin (CT present) AND cranial nerve territory (V1 direct
brainstem). Limb hairy skin has CT but no direct brainstem access;
glabrous skin has neither CT nor brainstem access.
→ Cross-ref: C01 §4.7

⚡ **Framework note 1 — CONFIRMS cranial nerve architecture pattern
(extends GV20 note 2).** Yintang = PURE V1 pathway (supratrochlear
nerve only, no C2 contribution like GV20). The eTNS FDA approval at the
SAME nerve territory (V1 supratrochlear + supraorbital) validates this
neural pathway for therapeutic effect with a different modality
(electrical vs mechanical). Combined with GV20 (V1+C2), this confirms
that head-region points operate through a fundamentally different
neural architecture from limb/trunk points: cranial nerve → brainstem
DIRECT, no spinal cord intermediary. The emerging pattern across Phase
1: all head-region points tested (GV20, now Yintang) show direct
brainstem access. Batch 11 (auricular points) will test whether the
auricular branch of vagus nerve (ABVN) vagal pathway represents a third variant of this cranial nerve
architecture advantage.

⚡ **Framework note 2 — eTNS-acupuncture pathway convergence.** The
Cefaly eTNS device and Yintang acupuncture/acupressure target the SAME
nerves (supratrochlear, supraorbital) at the SAME anatomical location
(forehead/glabella). eTNS is FDA-approved; Yintang has 5 dedicated
randomized controlled trials for anxiety. This is the clearest
example in the Atlas of a traditional acupoint whose neural pathway has
been independently validated by a modern medical device — not as
evidence FOR acupuncture, but as evidence that this specific neural
pathway IS therapeutically exploitable. C04 should consider adding
eTNS-acupoint pathway convergence as a new validation category alongside
segmental convergence and cross-tradition convergence.

#### Kỹ thuật kích thích + dose-response

Châm (B01): 3-8 mm (0.3-0.5 cun) transverse/oblique insertion.
Direction: inferiorly toward nose bridge or superiorly toward GV24.
Technique: pinching the skin between thumb and index finger before
insertion controls depth and prevents needle slipping on the curved
bony surface. De Qi sensation: distension and heaviness at the
forehead, sometimes radiating to the nose bridge. Thin needle
recommended (0.25 mm diameter). Retention: 15-30 minutes standard.
  Electroacupuncture (EA): GV20+Yintang is the most common pair for EA in
  depression/insomnia protocols. Low frequency (2 Hz) for calming
  effect (endorphin release, Han's frequency model). Zheng et al.
  2012 used 30-minute EA at GV20+Yintang.

Bấm huyệt (B02): thumb or fingertip pressure at the glabella.
Moderate pressure with circular or sustained technique. Duration:
1-3 minutes. Self-applicable — this is one of the most accessible
acupressure points for self-treatment. 4 of 5 randomized controlled
trials in Kwon & Lee 2018 used single-point intervention (acupuncture
or acupressure at Yintang alone). Preoperative protocol: 20 minutes of
acupressure produced significant State-Trait Anxiety Inventory
reduction.

Cứu (B08): not commonly used at Yintang. Proximity to eyebrows and
eyelashes creates fire risk with direct or indirect moxibustion. If
used, only with moxa stick (indirect) at safe distance. Not standard
clinical practice.

Dry needling (B11): not standard at this location. No established
trigger point dry needling protocol for procerus/corrugator, though
these muscles can develop trigger points. Botulinum toxin injection at
the same muscles (for cosmetic purposes) demonstrates that the
procerus/corrugator are accessible to needle intervention.

So sánh: acupuncture and acupressure are both supported by evidence at
Yintang (both included in Kwon & Lee 2018 review). Acupressure has the
advantage of self-applicability and safety. Electroacupuncture
(GV20+Yintang pair) is the standard for depression/insomnia research
protocols. Moxibustion is NOT standard at this point.

#### An toàn

**Bruising** = primary concern. The supratrochlear artery and vein are
superficial at the glabella. Bruising is common and cosmetically visible
(between the eyebrows). Patients should be informed of bruising risk
before treatment. Thin needles and transverse insertion minimize
vascular trauma.

**Infection risk:** standard clean needle technique applies. The
forehead skin is well-vascularized with good immune response — infection
risk is low with proper technique.

**No pneumothorax risk** (facial point — no thoracic cavity access).

**No major vascular risk** beyond superficial bruising. Internal carotid
branches (ophthalmic artery) are deep within the orbit, not accessible
at standard needle depth.

**Pregnancy:** NOT contraindicated. Yintang is not among the
traditionally pregnancy-restricted points (LI4, SP6, BL67, sacral
points).

**Electroacupuncture caution:** when using electroacupuncture at
Yintang, current may spread to temporal branches of facial nerve
(cranial nerve VII motor fibers), causing involuntary periorbital muscle twitching.
Adjust current intensity to avoid motor nerve activation. Avoid
crossing electrodes across the eyes.

> confirmed at TWO Phase 1 points (GV20: V1+C2, Yintang: pure V1).
> eTNS FDA approval validates the pathway. Next test: auricular branch of vagus nerve (ABVN)
> pathway (Batch 11) — third cranial nerve variant of the head-region
> architecture advantage.
>
> *Next: Batch 11 — Auricular points (Shen Men, Sympathetic, Point
> Zero), AP-16-Auricular.*

---

### EX-HN1 — Tứ Thần Thông (四神聪, Sìshéncōng)

**Evidence tier: B (Standard Entry)**

"Four Spirit Alertness" (四 = four, 神 = spirit, 聪 = alertness/
intelligence). A group of 4 points arranged around GV20 (Baihui) at 1
cun distance in the 4 cardinal directions (anterior, posterior, left,
right). Spirit-name point (神 in name) — joins a select group of
acupoints referencing 神: GV24 Shenting (神庭), HT7 Shenmen (神门),
BL44 Shentang (神堂), KI25 Shencang (神藏), CV8 Shenque (神阙). The
most-used non-meridian point group for neuropsychiatric conditions
after Yintang. GV20+Sishencong+KI3 = PSCI (post-stroke cognitive
impairment) protocol.

#### Vị trí giải phẫu

WHO standard location (WPRO 2008): a group of 4 points on the vertex,
each 1 cun from GV20 (Baihui), in the anterior, posterior, left, and
right directions.

Individual point locations:
- **Anterior point:** 1 cun anterior to GV20, on the midline. Between
  GV20 and GV21 (Qianding). V1 territory.
- **Posterior point:** 1 cun posterior to GV20, on the midline. Between
  GV20 and GV19 (Houding). C2 territory.
- **Left and right points:** 1 cun lateral to GV20, on the coronal
  plane. No standard meridian point at this location. V3 territory.

All 4 points sit on the calvarium (skull vault). The galea
aponeurotica (epicranial aponeurosis) connects all 4 points as a
continuous fascial sheet.

Needle depth: 0.5–0.8 cun (1.2–2.0 cm) oblique or transverse,
typically directed toward GV20. Subcutaneous insertion beneath the
galea — the calvarium lies directly beneath.

#### Cấu trúc mô học tại điểm

Nerve supply varies by point location:
- **Anterior point:** supraorbital nerve (V1 ophthalmic division) and
  anterior branch of superficial temporal artery. Same V1 territory
  as GV20's anterior innervation.
- **Posterior point:** greater occipital nerve (C2 dorsal ramus).
  Same C2 territory as GV20's posterior innervation.
- **Lateral points:** auriculotemporal nerve (V3 mandibular division)
  and branches of superficial temporal artery. V3 territory adds a
  dimension not prominent at GV20 midline.
- All points: parietal branches of middle meningeal artery (deep,
  intracranial — separated by calvarium).

Spinal segments: mixed cranial nerve (V1, V3) + C2. No spinal cord
relay — trigeminal afferents project directly to brainstem trigeminal
sensory nuclear complex.

Fascial plane: skin → subcutaneous tissue → galea aponeurotica
(epicranial aponeurosis) → loose areolar tissue (subgaleal space) →
pericranium → calvarium.
→ Cross-ref: A01 S1.4, A03, AP-13 GV20 (identical scalp anatomy)

Vascular: superficial temporal artery branches (all 4 points). The
scalp is highly vascularized — hemostasis may be needed after
needling.

#### Phân loại huyệt (Point Category Membership)

Extra point (经外奇穴): EX-HN1 in WHO nomenclature. Not on any
meridian.

Five Shu: not applicable. Yuan-Source: not applicable.
Luo-Connecting: not applicable. Xi-Cleft: not applicable.
Back-Shu: not applicable. Front-Mu: not applicable.
Eight Confluent: not applicable. Hui-Meeting: not applicable.

Special designations:
- **Spirit-name point (神穴):** 神聪 (Shen Cong, "Spirit Alertness")
  contains 神 = spirit. Spirit-name points traditionally target
  neuropsychiatric conditions: consciousness, cognition, emotion,
  sleep. Other 神-points: HT7 神门, GV24 神庭, BL44 神堂, KI25 神藏,
  CV8 神阙.
- **Multi-point group:** 4 individual points treated as a functional
  unit. All 4 needled simultaneously in standard clinical practice.
- **GV20 satellite system:** Sishencong creates a "crown" of
  stimulation around GV20, expanding the area of neural activation
  beyond the single midline point.
→ Cross-ref: B01 S1.3.3 (extra point classification)

#### Cross-tradition mapping

**1. TCM acupoint:** EX-HN1 (Sishencong 四神聪, "Four Spirit
Alertness"). Functions: calms the spirit (安神), benefits intelligence
(益智), clears the head (清头). Primary indications: insomnia,
dizziness, poor memory, headache (vertex), epilepsy, mania, tinnitus.
The spirit-calming function parallels GV20 but with a broader
stimulation field. ✓

**2. Trigger point:** the temporalis and occipitalis muscles develop
trigger points that refer pain to the vertex and temporal regions.
The epicranial aponeurosis itself can develop taut bands associated
with tension-type headache. Sishencong's 4 points span temporalis
(lateral) and occipitalis (posterior) territories, accessing common
headache trigger points. 🟡

**3. Ashi correspondence:** vertex tenderness is a common finding in
tension-type headache and migraine. Patients report diffuse vertex
pressure or a "band" around the head. Sishencong's 4-point crown
pattern corresponds to common palpation tenderness at the vertex. 🟡

**4. Marma point:** Adhipati Marma (अधिपति, "Overlord") — the vertex
Marma, located at the crown of the head. Classified as Sandhi (joint)
Marma in some texts. Size: ½ anguli. Injury: Sadyah Pranahara
(lethal). Adhipati is at GV20's location; Sishencong surrounds it at
~1 cun (2.5 cm) distance. The vertex Adhipati zone functionally
overlaps with the Sishencong+GV20 area. 🟡

**5. Head zone:** V1 (anterior point) + V3 (lateral points) + C2
(posterior point) dermatomes. No viscerotome projection — cranial
territory does not project to specific internal organs via
dermatome-viscerotome convergence. Same finding as GV20 and Yintang:
therapeutic effects on cognition, sleep, and mood cannot be explained
by segmental convergence. 🟡

**6. Anatomy Trains:** Superficial Back Line (SBL) and Superficial
Front Line (SFL) both terminate at the galea aponeurotica on the
vertex. All 4 Sishencong points sit on the galea — the fascial
crossroads where SBL (posterior approach via erector spinae → nuchal
ligament → epicranial fascia) and SFL (anterior approach via SCM →
scalp fascia) meet. The lateral points also contact the Lateral Line
terminus. 🟡

**7. Neurogenic spot:** no data. Kim 2017 mapped neurogenic
inflammatory spots on trunk dermatomes (T1-L2); cranial/vertex
regions were not included. ✗

**8. Cupping point:** not applicable. The convex scalp surface and
hair growth make cupping impractical at the vertex. ✗

**Convergence score: 6/8** (TCM ✓, Trigger point 🟡, Ashi 🟡, Marma
🟡, Head zone 🟡, Anatomy Trains 🟡). High convergence for a vertex
extra point — consistent with GV20's high convergence at the same
anatomical region. The vertex is a multi-tradition recognized zone for
neuropsychiatric intervention.

#### Chỉ định truyền thống (DATA — ghi nhận, không endorse as mechanism)

Nguồn cổ:
- Extra point collections (Qianjin Yaofang, Sun Si-miao 652 CE, and
  later compilations): Sishencong for "calming the spirit, clearing
  the mind, treating vertex headache, dizziness, epilepsy, and mania."
  Not in the earliest classical sources (Neijing, Nanjing) which
  focused on channel points.

Nguồn hiện đại (Deadman, WHO):
- Insomnia, poor memory, dizziness, vertex headache, epilepsy, mania
- Tinnitus, blurred vision
- Often combined with GV20 — the 5-point vertex combination
  (GV20 + 4×Sishencong) is a standard neuropsychiatric protocol

Kinh nghiệm lâm sàng nổi bật:
- **PSCI protocol:** GV20 + Sishencong + KI3 is a studied combination
  for post-stroke cognitive impairment. Multiple clinical trials in
  Chinese acupuncture literature use this specific 3-component
  protocol.
- **Insomnia:** GV20 + Sishencong + HT7 + SP6 is a standard
  insomnia protocol. The 5-point vertex cluster provides broad vertex
  coverage for calming and cognitive effects.
- **Scalp acupuncture precursor:** the Sishencong group may be an
  early form of scalp acupuncture — using vertex points for
  neuropsychiatric conditions foreshadows modern scalp acupuncture
  systems (Jiao, YNSA) that systematically map cortical projections
  to scalp zones.
→ Cross-ref: AP-13 GV20 (L4713, 4761, 4772 — PSCI protocol),
AP-05 HT7 (insomnia protocol)

#### Bằng chứng hiện đại

**① Post-stroke cognitive impairment (PSCI) — 🟡 Plausible**

GV20+Sishencong+KI3 protocol studied in multiple Chinese RCTs for
PSCI. Typical design: EA at GV20+Sishencong + manual acupuncture at
KI3, 3–5×/week for 4–8 weeks. Outcomes: Mini-Mental State Examination
(MMSE), Montreal Cognitive Assessment (MoCA) improvement reported.

Limitations: most studies published in Chinese journals; small sample
sizes; variable sham/control conditions; Sishencong never tested alone
(always part of multi-point protocol). The GV20+Sishencong combination
is logical (expanding vertex stimulation area) but individual
Sishencong contribution is not isolated.

**② Insomnia — 🟡 Plausible**

Included in standard insomnia protocols (GV20+Sishencong+HT7+SP6).
Same limitations as PSCI: always in combination, never sole-point
evidence.

**③ Epilepsy — 🔴 Unvalidated**

Classical indication with no modern controlled evidence. Limited
case reports in Chinese literature.

#### Dynamic acupoint properties

No specific disease-state sensitization data for Sishencong. The
vertex scalp (GV20 area) shows increased tenderness in tension-type
headache and migraine, suggesting pericranial sensitization extends to
the Sishencong locations during headache states. No controlled
measurement studies.
→ Cross-ref: C04 S4 (Dynamic Acupoint Phenomenon)

#### Cơ chế giải thích (BSA framework attribution)

**Primary pathway: cranial nerve → brainstem direct (same architecture
as GV20).**

The 4 Sishencong points access THREE cranial/upper cervical nerve
territories simultaneously:
- Anterior point: V1 supraorbital → trigeminal brainstem nuclei
- Posterior point: C2 greater occipital → cervical dorsal horn +
  trigeminocervical complex
- Lateral points: V3 auriculotemporal → trigeminal brainstem nuclei

Combined with GV20 (V1+C2), the 5-point cluster creates maximum
trigeminal-cervical activation at the vertex. All pathways converge
on the trigeminocervical complex, which connects to NTS, LC, DRN, and
PAG — the same brainstem targets identified for GV20 and Yintang.

**Expanded coverage model:** Sishencong adds V3 territory (lateral
points) that GV20 alone does not strongly access. The auriculotemporal
nerve (V3) carries sensory fibers from the temporal region and also
postganglionic parasympathetic fibers from the otic ganglion to the
parotid gland. Whether V3 stimulation at the lateral Sishencong points
activates parasympathetic pathways via otic ganglion connections is
unknown.

**Cognitive effects mechanism:** PSCI protocol efficacy (if confirmed)
would suggest that vertex stimulation affects cortical function through
ascending brainstem-cortical pathways (LC norepinephrine, DRN
serotonin → cortical arousal/plasticity). Direct transcranial effects
on underlying cortex are speculative — needle depth is subcutaneous,
well above the calvarium.

Gap: no identified molecular effector for Sishencong specifically. The
PSCI protocol's cognitive mechanism is not established.
→ Cross-ref: AP-13 GV20 (cranial nerve architecture), A01 S1.4, A02

#### Kỹ thuật kích thích + dose-response

Châm (B01): 0.5–0.8 cun (1.2–2.0 cm) oblique or transverse
insertion. Direction: typically toward GV20 (converging needles create
maximum stimulation density at vertex center). Some protocols direct
needles AWAY from GV20 for broader area coverage. Thin needles
(0.25 mm). Retention: 20–30 minutes.

All 4 points typically needled simultaneously — this is the standard
protocol. Single-point Sishencong use is uncommon.

EA: GV20-to-anterior Sishencong is a common electrode pair. 2 Hz for
calming/cognitive effect. 30-minute stimulation typical for PSCI
protocols.

Bấm huyệt (B02): fingertip pressure at all 4 points sequentially or
simultaneously. Self-massage of the vertex area functionally
stimulates the Sishencong zone.

Cứu (B08): not standard. Hair on vertex makes direct moxa
impractical. Moxa stick at distance occasionally used.

Dry needling (B11): not standard for this location.
→ Cross-ref: B01 S3.1, B02

#### An toàn

**Subgaleal hematoma:** the loose areolar tissue (subgaleal space) of
the scalp allows blood to spread widely if vessels are damaged.
Patients on anticoagulants have elevated risk. Apply pressure after
needle removal.

**Fontanelle (infants):** in infants and young children, the anterior
fontanelle (at/near the anterior Sishencong location) and posterior
fontanelle (near posterior location) may not be fully closed. Needling
is CONTRAINDICATED before fontanelle closure (typically by 18–24
months).

**No pneumothorax risk** (cranial location).

**Pregnancy:** NOT contraindicated. Vertex points are not among
traditionally pregnancy-restricted points.

---

### EX-HN2 — Đương Dương (当阳, Dāngyáng)

**Evidence tier: C (Minimum Viable Entry)**

"Facing the Sun" (当 = facing, 阳 = sun/yang). On the head, 1 cun
above the anterior hairline, directly above the pupil center. A minor
forehead/scalp point for headache, dizziness, and eye pain. V1
supraorbital territory.

#### Vị trí giải phẫu

WHO standard location (WPRO 2008): on the head, 1 cun above the
anterior hairline, directly above the pupil when looking straight
ahead.

Anatomical landmarks: 0.5 cun posterior to GB15 (Toulinqi), on the
same vertical line passing through the pupil center. On the frontal
bone, beneath the scalp.

Needle depth: 0.5–0.8 cun transverse/oblique insertion. Same scalp
layer structure as other vertex/frontal points.

#### Cấu trúc mô học tại điểm

Nerve: supraorbital nerve (V1 ophthalmic division, frontal nerve
branch). Spinal segment: none (cranial nerve territory).
Vascular: frontal branch of superficial temporal artery.
Fascia: galea aponeurotica. Muscle: frontalis.
→ Cross-ref: A01 S1.4

#### Phân loại huyệt

Extra point (EX-HN2). No meridian category memberships.

Nearby meridian points: GB15 (Toulinqi) 0.5 cun anterior on the same
pupil-vertical line. BL4 (Qucha) nearby but more medial.

#### Cross-tradition mapping

TCM: EX-HN2 (Dangyang 当阳). Headache (frontal, vertex), dizziness,
eye pain, nasal congestion. ✓
Trigger point: frontalis muscle trigger point in this region refers
pain to the forehead. 🟡
Ashi: forehead/scalp tenderness possible in tension-type headache. 🟡
Marma: no specific Marma at this location. ✗
Head zone: V1 dermatome, no viscerotome (cranial territory). ✗
Anatomy Trains: galea aponeurotica, SFL/SBL terminus region —
limited significance. ✗
Neurogenic spot: no data (Kim 2017 = trunk only). ✗
Cupping: not applicable (scalp). ✗

**Convergence score: 3/8** (TCM ✓, Trigger point 🟡, Ashi 🟡). Low
convergence — a minor extra point with limited cross-tradition
recognition.

#### Chỉ định truyền thống

Classical sources (extra point compilations): frontal headache,
dizziness, blurred vision, eye pain, nasal congestion. Rarely used as
a standalone point — usually substituted by nearby meridian points
(GB15, GV24, BL4) which have stronger channel-theory backing.

#### Bằng chứng hiện đại

No systematic review or RCT specifically studying EX-HN2. Usually
substituted by nearby meridian points in modern practice.

#### An toàn

Standard scalp needling risks. Subgaleal bleeding in anticoagulated
patients. No pneumothorax risk. Not pregnancy-contraindicated.

---

### EX-HN4 — Ngư Yêu (鱼腰, Yúyāo)

**Evidence tier: C (Minimum Viable Entry)**

"Fish Waist" (鱼 = fish, 腰 = waist) — the midpoint of the eyebrow,
named because the eyebrow shape resembles a fish and this point sits
at its center. Directly above the pupil. Part of the periorbital trio:
BL2 (Zanzhu, medial eyebrow) + EX-HN4 (Yuyao, center eyebrow) +
TE23 (Sizhukong, lateral eyebrow) — 3 points along the eyebrow that
collectively cover V1 ophthalmic territory for eye and periorbital
disorders.

#### Vị trí giải phẫu

WHO standard location (WPRO 2008): on the forehead, at the midpoint
of the eyebrow, directly above the pupil when looking straight ahead.

Anatomical landmarks: palpate along the eyebrow to its midpoint;
confirm alignment by asking the patient to look straight ahead and
verifying the point is directly above the pupil. Sits between BL2
(medial end of eyebrow) and TE23 (lateral end of eyebrow).

Needle depth: 0.3–0.5 cun transverse along the eyebrow (directed
medially toward BL2 or laterally toward TE23). Perpendicular insertion
not standard — the supraorbital ridge and orbit lie directly beneath.

#### Cấu trúc mô học tại điểm

Nerve: supraorbital nerve (V1 ophthalmic division) — exits through
the supraorbital foramen or notch at approximately this location.
EX-HN4 may sit directly over the supraorbital foramen in many
individuals. Spinal segment: none (cranial nerve V1).
Motor: temporal branch of facial nerve (CN VII) — frontalis and
corrugator supercilii.
Vascular: supraorbital artery and vein.
Fascia: superficial fascia of the forehead. Muscle layers: skin →
orbicularis oculi (superior fibers) → corrugator supercilii →
periosteum of frontal bone / supraorbital ridge.
→ Cross-ref: A01 S1.4

#### Phân loại huyệt

Extra point (EX-HN4). No meridian category memberships.

**Periorbital trio:** BL2 (Zanzhu, medial) + EX-HN4 (Yuyao, center)
+ TE23 (Sizhukong, lateral). This 3-point combination spans the
entire eyebrow for eye and periorbital conditions.
→ Cross-ref: AP-11 GB (L2578), AP-10 TE (L5562, L5737, L5873)

#### Cross-tradition mapping

TCM: EX-HN4 (Yuyao 鱼腰). Eye disorders (redness, swelling, pain,
lacrimation), ptosis, supraorbital pain, eyelid twitching, facial
paralysis (Bell's palsy). ✓
Trigger point: corrugator supercilii trigger point refers pain to the
medial forehead and eyebrow. Orbicularis oculi trigger point at the
superior orbital rim. 🟡
Ashi: supraorbital tenderness common in frontal sinusitis and tension
headache. 🟡
Marma: no specific eyebrow midpoint Marma. ✗
Head zone: V1 dermatome, no viscerotome. ✗
Anatomy Trains: periorbital muscles are not primary components of
myofascial meridian chains — limited significance. ✗
Neurogenic spot: no data (Kim 2017 = trunk only). ✗
Cupping: not applicable (periorbital). ✗

**Convergence score: 3/8** (TCM ✓, Trigger point 🟡, Ashi 🟡). Low
convergence, typical for minor periorbital extra points.

#### Chỉ định truyền thống

Classical and modern sources: supraorbital pain, eye redness and
swelling, ptosis (drooping eyelid), eyelid twitching, lacrimation,
blurred vision, facial paralysis affecting the forehead branch (CN VII
temporal branch palsy = inability to raise eyebrow).

Clinical: commonly used in combination with BL2 and TE23 (periorbital
trio) for eye disorders and facial paralysis. Part of standard Bell's
palsy acupuncture protocols: ST4+ST6+LI4+GB14+BL2+Yuyao+TE23.

→ Cross-ref: AP-10 TE23 (periorbital protocols), AP-11 GB14

#### Bằng chứng hiện đại

No systematic review or RCT specifically studying EX-HN4 as standalone
point. Appears in Bell's palsy and eye disorder protocols as part of the
periorbital trio (BL2+Yuyao+TE23) — not individually evaluated.

#### An toàn

**Orbital proximity:** the supraorbital ridge and orbit lie directly
beneath the eyebrow. Deep perpendicular needling risks orbital entry.
Use transverse insertion along the eyebrow plane only.

**Supraorbital neurovascular bundle:** the supraorbital artery, vein,
and nerve exit together through the supraorbital foramen/notch at this
location. Bruising is common.

**Eye safety:** needle tip must be directed along the eyebrow axis,
not inferiorly toward the globe. Patients should keep eyes closed
during treatment.

Not pregnancy-contraindicated.

---

### EX-HN5 — Thái Dương (太阳, Tàiyáng)

**Evidence tier: B (Standard Entry)**

"Great Yang" (太 = great, 阳 = yang/sun). The temple point — in the
depression posterior to the midpoint between the lateral end of the
eyebrow and the outer canthus. The MOST clinically used temporal
headache point across all acupuncture traditions, and arguably the
most intuitive — patients instinctively press their temples during
headaches. A V2+V3 junction point unique among head extra points
(Yintang = pure V1, GV20 = V1+C2, Sishencong = V1+V3+C2). Highest
cross-tradition convergence of any head/neck extra point (7/8).
Classical bloodletting site: pricking the superficial temporal vein
at Taiyang is one of the oldest acupuncture techniques for acute
headache and congestion.

#### Vị trí giải phẫu

WHO standard location (WPRO 2008): on the head, at the temple, in
the depression about 1 cun posterior to the midpoint of a line
connecting the lateral end of the eyebrow and the outer canthus.

Anatomical landmarks: palpate the temporal fossa — the depression
behind the lateral orbital rim. The point lies in the most palpable
depression when the jaw is relaxed. The superficial temporal artery
and vein are palpable at or near this point.

Needle depth: 0.3–0.5 cun (0.8–1.2 cm) perpendicular or oblique
posteriorly. For bloodletting: superficial prick with triangular
needle or lancet at the visible superficial temporal vein.

#### Cấu trúc mô học tại điểm

Nerve supply:
- **Zygomaticotemporal nerve** (branch of V2 maxillary division via
  zygomatic nerve) — sensory to the temple skin. Passes through the
  lateral orbital wall and emerges through the zygomaticotemporal
  foramen on the temporal fossa.
- **Auriculotemporal nerve** (branch of V3 mandibular division) —
  sensory to the temporal region, ear, and TMJ. Carries
  postganglionic parasympathetic fibers from the otic ganglion to
  the parotid gland.
- **Temporal branch of facial nerve (CN VII)** — motor to frontalis,
  orbicularis oculi. Crosses the zygomatic arch superficially.
- Spinal segments: none (cranial nerve territory, V2+V3).

The V2+V3 junction makes Taiyang unique among head extra points:

| Point | Nerve territory | Pathway |
|---|---|---|
| Yintang (EX-HN3) | Pure V1 | Supratrochlear → brainstem |
| GV20 | V1 + C2 | Supraorbital + greater occipital |
| Sishencong (EX-HN1) | V1 + V3 + C2 | Crown coverage |
| **Taiyang (EX-HN5)** | **V2 + V3** | **Zygomaticotemporal + auriculotemporal** |

V2 significance: V2 traverses the pterygopalatine fossa, where the
pterygopalatine ganglion serves as the major parasympathetic relay
for lacrimal gland, nasal mucosa, and palatal glands. The V2
connection at Taiyang may provide indirect access to this
parasympathetic relay.

Fascial plane: temporal fascia (deep to skin, superficial to
temporalis muscle). The temporalis is a powerful jaw-closing muscle —
tension in temporalis is a major contributor to temporal headache and
TMJ dysfunction.

Vascular: superficial temporal artery (terminal branch of external
carotid) — the most accessible major artery in the head, with a
palpable pulse. Superficial temporal vein — visible through skin in
lean individuals. Middle temporal artery lies deeper, between
temporalis layers.
→ Cross-ref: A01 S1.4, A03

#### Phân loại huyệt (Point Category Membership)

Extra point (经外奇穴): EX-HN5 in WHO nomenclature. Not on any
meridian, though it lies between the Gallbladder (Shaoyang) and
Triple Energizer (Shaoyang) meridian territories — both Shaoyang
channels traverse the temporal region.

Five Shu: not applicable. Yuan-Source: not applicable.
Luo-Connecting: not applicable. Xi-Cleft: not applicable.
Back-Shu: not applicable. Front-Mu: not applicable.
Eight Confluent: not applicable. Hui-Meeting: not applicable.

Special designations:
- **Most-used temporal headache point** — included in virtually all
  acupuncture headache/migraine protocols for the temporal region.
- **Classical bloodletting point** — pricking the superficial temporal
  vein at Taiyang is documented across centuries of Chinese medical
  literature.
- **Shaoyang territory marker:** though not ON the GB or TE meridians,
  Taiyang lies in their territory. Shaoyang headache (temporal,
  one-sided) is the most common headache pattern in TCM, and Taiyang
  is the primary local point.
→ Cross-ref: AP-11 GB (L1496, 1617, 2828, 9625 — Shaoyang migraine
protocols), AP-10 TE5 (L405)

#### Cross-tradition mapping

**1. TCM acupoint:** EX-HN5 (Taiyang 太阳, "Great Yang"). Functions:
clears Heat, brightens the eyes, relieves pain, dispels Wind. Primary
indications: temporal headache, migraine (especially Shaoyang-type
one-sided headache), eye disorders, facial paralysis, toothache
(upper), trigeminal neuralgia. The MOST commonly used local point for
temporal headache — included in Shaoyang protocols alongside GB20,
TE5, GB41. ✓

**2. Trigger point:** temporalis muscle trigger points are among the
most clinically significant for headache. The anterior and middle
fibers at the temple are common trigger point locations. Referred
pain: temporal headache, upper tooth pain (V2 maxillary territory
overlap), retro-orbital pain. Travell & Simons identify the
temporalis as a primary contributor to tension-type headache and TMJ
dysfunction. Taiyang sits directly over the anterior-middle
temporalis. ✓

**3. Ashi correspondence:** the temple is THE cardinal self-identified
headache point. Patients with temporal headache instinctively press
their temples for relief — a universal pain behavior across cultures.
Temple tenderness is a standard palpation finding in tension-type
headache, migraine, and TMJ dysfunction. ✓

**4. Marma point:** Shankha Marma (शंख, "Conch Shell") — the temple
Marma. Located in the temporal region, on the thin temporal bone. One
of 107 Marma points. Classification: Asthi (bone) Marma. Size: ½
anguli. Injury consequence: Sadyah Pranahara (immediately lethal) —
reflecting the thin temporal bone with the middle meningeal artery in
a groove on its internal surface. Therapeutic use: headache, stress,
jaw tension, eye disorders. Strong correspondence with Taiyang in
both location and therapeutic application. ✓

**5. Head zone:** V2 (maxillary) + V3 (mandibular) dermatome
territory. V2 viscerotome: maxillary sinus, nasal cavity, palate
(via pterygopalatine ganglion connections). V3: teeth, TMJ,
masticatory muscles. The temple sits at the V2-V3 junction — pain
from upper teeth (V2) and lower jaw (V3) can both refer to the
temporal region. 🟡

**6. Anatomy Trains:** the temporalis muscle is part of the Deep Front
Line (DFL) of the head — the masticatory complex extending from
mandible through temporalis to the temporal fascia. The Lateral Line
has connections through the SCM-temporal fascia continuum. 🟡

**7. Neurogenic spot:** no data (Kim 2017 studied trunk dermatomes
only). ✗

**8. Cupping point:** small cup bloodletting (刺血拔罐) at Taiyang is
a classical Chinese medical technique. A triangular needle or lancet
pricks the superficial temporal vein, followed by application of a
small cup to extract blood. Used for acute headache, eye redness,
facial swelling, and Heat conditions. One of the most well-documented
point-specific cupping techniques in TCM. 🟡

**Convergence score: 7/8** (TCM ✓, Trigger point ✓, Ashi ✓, Marma ✓,
Head zone 🟡, Anatomy Trains 🟡, Cupping 🟡). Very high convergence
— the HIGHEST of any extra point documented so far, and among the
highest in the entire Atlas. The temple is a universal headache
management site across all medical traditions. Only neurogenic spot
data is missing (Kim 2017 did not study cranial regions).

#### Chỉ định truyền thống (DATA — ghi nhận, không endorse as mechanism)

Nguồn cổ:
- Classical texts (Neijing supplements, Qianjin Yaofang, Zhenjiu
  Dacheng): Taiyang for temporal headache, migraine, eye redness and
  pain, toothache, facial swelling. Bloodletting at Taiyang is one
  of the most frequently mentioned techniques in classical
  acupuncture literature.
- The name "Great Yang" (太阳) is significant — in Chinese cosmology,
  太阳 also refers to the Sun. The temple is where the temporal
  artery pulses visibly.

Nguồn hiện đại (Deadman, WHO):
- Headache (temporal, migraine, one-sided)
- Eye disorders: conjunctivitis, eye pain, lacrimation
- Facial paralysis
- Toothache (upper teeth)
- Trigeminal neuralgia

Kinh nghiệm lâm sàng nổi bật:
- **Shaoyang migraine protocol:** Taiyang + GB20 (Fengchi) + TE5
  (Waiguan) + GB41 (Zulinqi) — standard temporal headache
  combination. Taiyang = local point; GB20 = suboccipital access;
  TE5+GB41 = distal Shaoyang points.
- **Bloodletting:** pricking the superficial temporal vein followed
  by cup or cotton — one of the most enduring techniques in Chinese
  medicine. Used for acute presentations: sudden severe headache,
  acute conjunctivitis, high fever with facial flushing.
- **Periorbital combination:** Taiyang + BL2 + GB14 + TE23 for eye
  disorders — covers lateral (Taiyang), medial (BL2), superior
  (GB14), and lateral eyebrow (TE23) periorbital access.
→ Cross-ref: AP-11 GB (Shaoyang migraine protocols), AP-10 TE5 (L405)

#### Bằng chứng hiện đại

**① Migraine / tension-type headache — 🟡 Plausible**

Taiyang is included in multiple acupuncture headache RCTs and SRs,
but always as part of a multi-point protocol (typically with GB20,
LI4, TE5). Vickers et al. 2012 (BMJ) and 2018 (JAMA) individual
patient data meta-analyses showed acupuncture superior to sham for
chronic headache, but Taiyang's individual contribution is not
isolable. The Cochrane review for migraine prophylaxis (Linde et al.
2016) includes trials using Taiyang as a local temporal point, but
protocols vary.

**② Eye disorders — 🔴 Unvalidated**

Traditional indication with anatomical rationale (V2 proximity to
pterygopalatine ganglion → lacrimal/nasal connections) but no modern
controlled evidence specific to Taiyang for eye conditions.

**③ Bloodletting efficacy — 🔴 Unvalidated**

Despite being one of the most enduring classical techniques, Taiyang
bloodletting has no rigorous modern evidence. Difficult to
sham-control (patient knows whether blood was drawn). Mechanistically,
venous bleeding could reduce local congestion and provide nociceptive
input (DNIC effect), but not systematically studied.

#### Dynamic acupoint properties

Temporal region sensitization is well-documented in headache states:
- **Migraine:** temporal tenderness is a defining feature. Cephalic
  allodynia extends to the temporal skin — lowered mechanical pain
  threshold in V2+V3 territory.
- **Tension-type headache:** pericranial tenderness at the temple is
  both a diagnostic criterion and a pathophysiological mechanism
  (peripheral → central sensitization).
- **TMJ dysfunction:** temporalis muscle tenderness and palpation
  pain at the temple.

The Taiyang region becomes more sensitive (lower pressure pain
threshold) during active headache — among the best clinically
documented examples of regional sensitization at an extra point
location, though controlled threshold data specifically at the
Taiyang point is limited.
→ Cross-ref: C04 S4 (Dynamic Acupoint Phenomenon)

#### Cơ chế giải thích (BSA framework attribution)

**Primary pathway: V2+V3 trigeminal → brainstem.**

```
V2 zygomaticotemporal + V3 auriculotemporal afferents at temple
→ trigeminal ganglion → trigeminal sensory nuclear complex
  → Spinal trigeminal nucleus (SpV, caudal part):
    headache/pain processing via trigeminovascular system
  → Principal sensory nucleus (Vp):
    discriminative touch
  → Mesencephalic nucleus (Vme):
    proprioception from temporalis muscle
→ Downstream:
  → NTS (vagal integration) → parasympathetic modulation
  → PAG (periaqueductal gray) → descending pain inhibition
  → Trigeminocervical complex → convergence with C1-C3
```

**V2 pathway significance:** V2 traverses the pterygopalatine fossa,
where the pterygopalatine ganglion relays parasympathetic fibers to
lacrimal gland, nasal mucosa, and palatal glands. Stimulation at
Taiyang may activate V2 afferents that modulate pterygopalatine
ganglion activity — potentially explaining traditional indications for
lacrimation and nasal symptoms. This V2→pterygopalatine pathway is
distinct from V1→brainstem at Yintang and GV20.

**Temporalis muscle mechanism:** Taiyang overlies the temporalis.
Acupuncture into temporalis directly modulates muscle tension, releases
trigger points, and activates local A-delta afferents for gate-control
pain inhibition. The therapeutic effect may be substantially LOCAL
(muscle relaxation + gate control) rather than requiring distant
brainstem relay.

**Bloodletting mechanism:** superficial temporal vein drainage may
reduce local venous congestion. During migraine and acute headache,
temporal arterial distension is visible. Venous bloodletting could
reduce local intravascular pressure and provide nociceptive input
(counter-irritant via DNIC, diffuse noxious inhibitory control).

Gap: V2→pterygopalatine parasympathetic pathway is anatomically
plausible but not experimentally verified for acupuncture. No molecular
effector identified.
→ Cross-ref: A01 S1.4, A02, A07

#### Kỹ thuật kích thích + dose-response

Châm (B01): 0.3–0.5 cun (0.8–1.2 cm) perpendicular or oblique
posteriorly. De Qi: distending sensation at the temple, may radiate to
the periorbital region. Avoid the superficial temporal artery —
palpate for pulse before insertion and needle adjacent to the artery.
Thin needle (0.25 mm). EA: less commonly used at Taiyang alone;
if paired, typically with GB20 (2 Hz for headache).

Thích huyết (bloodletting, B01): prick the visible superficial
temporal vein with a triangular needle (三棱针) or lancet. Allow 3–5
drops of blood, then apply cotton pressure. Optionally follow with
small cup for 1–2 minutes. Used for acute presentations: severe
headache, acute conjunctivitis, facial swelling, high fever.

Bấm huyệt (B02): thumb or fingertip pressure at the temple
depression. Circular or sustained pressure, moderate intensity.
Duration: 1–2 minutes per side, bilaterally. One of the most
naturally self-applied acupressure points — temple massage is a
universal headache response.

Cứu (B08): not commonly used at Taiyang. Proximity to superficial
temporal artery and thin temporal skin makes moxa less practical.

Dry needling (B11): temporalis muscle dry needling for TMJ dysfunction
and tension-type headache is standard practice. Taiyang overlies the
anterior-middle temporalis — a common dry needling target. Local
twitch response may be elicited.
→ Cross-ref: B01 S3.1, B02, B11

#### An toàn

**Superficial temporal artery:** PALPABLE at this location. Needle
insertion must avoid the artery — palpate for pulse before needling.
Accidental arterial puncture causes significant bruising and potential
temporal hematoma.

**CN VII temporal branch:** the temporal branch of the facial nerve
crosses the zygomatic arch superficially. Deep or aggressive needling
could potentially affect this motor nerve, causing transient weakness
of frontalis or orbicularis oculi. Risk is low at standard depth but
exists.

**Middle meningeal artery:** lies DEEP to the temporal bone — not
accessible at standard acupuncture depth. Only relevant in extreme
trauma to the thin temporal bone.

**Bloodletting-specific risks:** anticoagulated patients should NOT
receive bloodletting at Taiyang. Verify no bleeding disorders before
pricking. Apply adequate hemostasis.

**Pregnancy:** NOT contraindicated.

---

*Phase 2 Batch 1 COMPLETE. 4 entries added: EX-HN1 (Sishencong,
Tier B, 6/8 convergence), EX-HN2 (Dangyang, Tier C), EX-HN4
(Yuyao, Tier C, periorbital trio member), EX-HN5 (Taiyang, Tier B,
7/8 convergence — highest of any extra point). Total entries in AP-15:
5/48. No new ⚡ framework notes (running total: 41). Key findings:
Taiyang V2+V3 junction point unique among head extra points; 7/8
convergence reflects universal cross-tradition recognition of the
temple as a headache management site.*

---

### EX-HN6 — Nhĩ Tiêm (耳尖, Ěrjiān)

**Evidence tier: B (Standard Entry)**

"Ear Apex" (耳 = ear, 尖 = tip/apex). At the apex of the auricle when
the ear is folded anteriorly. The premier BLOODLETTING extra point —
its primary clinical application is pricking with a three-edged needle
(三棱针) to release drops of blood, not standard needle retention.
Indications center on acute inflammatory and excess Heat conditions:
hordeolum (stye), acute conjunctivitis, fever, hypertension. The ONLY
extra point with multiple condition-specific meta-analyses (hordeolum,
hypertension, stroke) all examining the same technique (bloodletting).
Distinguished from auricular microsystem points (AP-16): Erjian is on
the external auricle surface, not part of the inner ear somatotopic
projection system.

#### Vị trí giải phẫu

WHO standard location (WPRO 2008): on the auricle, at the apex of
the ear, when the auricle is folded anteriorly.

Anatomical landmarks: fold the ear forward along its longitudinal
axis. The highest point of the folded auricular crease is EX-HN6.
On the superior helix — NOT on the tragus, concha, or antihelix.

Technique note: this is primarily a BLOODLETTING point. Standard
needle retention is not the typical application. For bloodletting:
prick with a three-edged needle or disposable lancet to 1–2 mm
depth, enough to penetrate skin and release blood.

#### Cấu trúc mô học tại điểm

Nerve supply:
- **Auriculotemporal nerve (V3 mandibular division):** innervates
  the anterosuperior and anteromedial auricle, including the ear
  apex on its anterior surface. Found in 80% of the crus helicis
  (Peuker & Filler 2002). Primary nerve at the anterior-facing
  ear apex.
- **Great auricular nerve (C2-C3, cervical plexus):** anterior
  branch innervates the helix, including superior and posterior
  helix. Primary nerve at the posterior-facing ear apex.
- **Auricular branch of vagus nerve (ABVN):** does NOT significantly
  innervate the ear apex. ABVN territory is predominantly the
  conchal bowl (cymba conchae 100%, cavum conchae 45%), tragus
  (45%), and external auditory canal (Peuker & Filler 2002, Butt
  et al. 2020). Minor contribution to crus helicis (~20%) cannot
  be categorically excluded, but the helix/apex is NOT within
  established ABVN distribution.

Spinal segments: V3 (cranial) + C2-C3 (cervical plexus). No spinal
cord relay for V3 afferents.

ABVN distinction: the therapeutic effects of ear apex bloodletting
are NOT mediated by vagal afferents. The mechanism operates through
V3 trigeminal and/or C2-C3 cervical pathways. This distinguishes
EX-HN6 from conchal auricular points (AP-16) which DO access the
ABVN for vagal stimulation.
→ Cross-ref: A01 S1.4, A02 S3.5 (ABVN vagal pathway)

Tissue: skin → subcutaneous tissue → auricular cartilage (elastic
cartilage). No muscle layer — the helix has skin directly overlying
perichondrium and elastic cartilage.

Vascular: branches of posterior auricular artery (from external
carotid) and superficial temporal artery. Small caliber vessels
appropriate for controlled bloodletting.

#### Phân loại huyệt (Point Category Membership)

Extra point (经外奇穴): EX-HN6 in WHO nomenclature. Not on any
meridian.

Five Shu: not applicable. Yuan-Source: not applicable.
Luo-Connecting: not applicable. Xi-Cleft: not applicable.
Back-Shu: not applicable. Front-Mu: not applicable.
Eight Confluent: not applicable. Hui-Meeting: not applicable.

Special designations:
- **Premier bloodletting extra point:** the MOST systematically
  studied bloodletting-specific acupoint. Pricking to release blood
  is its PRIMARY technique — unlike most acupoints where bloodletting
  is a secondary/alternative application.
- **NOT an auricular microsystem point:** EX-HN6 is classified under
  Extra Points, not under the auricular microsystem (AP-16). It is on
  the external ear SURFACE (helix apex), not mapped onto the auricular
  somatotopy (inverted fetus model). Auricular microsystem points use
  inner ear structures (concha, antihelix, tragus) for organ
  projection; Erjian uses the outer helix for a different therapeutic
  paradigm (bloodletting for Heat-clearing).
→ Cross-ref: B01 S1.3.3, B01 S3.3 (auricular acupuncture)

#### Cross-tradition mapping

**1. TCM acupoint:** EX-HN6 (Erjian 耳尖, "Ear Apex"). Functions:
clears Heat (清热), reduces swelling (消肿), brightens the eyes
(明目), lowers Blood Pressure (降压). Primary indications: hordeolum
(stye), acute conjunctivitis, fever, hypertension, migraine, sore
throat. The Heat-clearing function explains its use across
inflammatory conditions — TCM regards bloodletting as the most
direct technique to "release excess Heat from the Blood level." ✓

**2. Trigger point:** no correspondence. The ear apex is elastic
cartilage with skin — no skeletal muscle to develop trigger points.
Travell & Simons' system does not include auricular locations. ✗

**3. Ashi correspondence:** not a standard self-identified tender
point. Patients do not typically localize pain at the ear apex during
febrile or inflammatory states. The ear may become hyperemic (red,
warm) during fever, but this is vascular engorgement rather than
palpation-tenderness in the Ashi sense. ✗

**4. Marma point:** no correspondence. Vidhura Marma (विधुर) is the
nearest auricular Marma — located behind/below the ear (mastoid
region), not at the apex. Krikatika Marma is at the atlantooccipital
junction. Neither corresponds to the ear apex. ✗

**5. Head zone:** V3 auriculotemporal + C2-C3 great auricular. The
ear apex's clinical indications (stye, fever, hypertension) are not
mediated through dermatome-viscerotome convergence. No relevant Head
zone pathway. ✗

**6. Anatomy Trains:** no correspondence. The auricle is elastic
cartilage — not integrated into any of Myers' myofascial meridian
chains. ✗

**7. Neurogenic spot:** no data (Kim 2017 = trunk only). ✗

**8. Cupping point:** not applicable. The ear apex surface is too
small and irregular for cup application. ✗

**Convergence score: 1/8** (TCM ✓ only). Very low — the LOWEST of
any Tier B point in the Atlas. Erjian's clinical value derives
entirely from its TECHNIQUE (bloodletting) rather than anatomical
convergence across independent traditions. The cross-tradition
mapping system (designed for needling/pressure sites at multi-system
convergence zones) does not capture bloodletting-specific therapeutic
value. Tier B assignment is based on EVIDENCE (multiple MAs), not
convergence.

#### Chỉ định truyền thống (DATA — ghi nhận, không endorse as mechanism)

Nguồn cổ:
- Classical extra point collections: Erjian for "clearing Heat,
  reducing swelling, brightening the eyes." Bloodletting at the ear
  apex documented in Qianjin Yaofang (Sun Si-miao, 652 CE) and later
  compilations. One of the most frequently cited bloodletting locations
  in Chinese medical literature.
- Technique tradition: massage auricle until red and warm → sterilize
  → prick with three-edged needle (三棱针) → express 3–10 drops of
  blood → cotton pressure hemostasis.

Nguồn hiện đại (Deadman, WHO):
- Hordeolum (stye/chalazion)
- Acute conjunctivitis, eye redness
- Fever (historical, particularly in children)
- Hypertension (adjunctive)
- Headache, migraine
- Sore throat

Kinh nghiệm lâm sàng nổi bật:
- **Hordeolum (stye):** MOST studied indication. Cochrane review
  (Cheng et al. 2017) found 4/6 hordeolum acupuncture RCTs
  specifically used ear apex bloodletting — "the most common technique
  used in treating an acute hordeolum."
- **Dose-response evidence:** RCT demonstrated 100 μL (~6 drops) more
  effective than 50 μL (~3 drops) for stye pain reduction (Qiao et al.
  2020, n=108), establishing a quantifiable dose-response relationship
  for bloodletting volume.
- **Hypertension adjunct:** clinical tradition of ear apex bloodletting
  alongside antihypertensive medication for acute BP elevation.
→ Cross-ref: B01 S3.1 (bloodletting technique)

#### Bằng chứng hiện đại

**① Hordeolum (stye) — 🟡 Plausible**

Cochrane review (Cheng et al. 2017, 6 RCTs, 531 participants): 4/6
trials used ear apex bloodletting. Acupuncture vs topical antibiotics
alone: RR 3.60 (95% CI 1.34–9.70). Combined acupuncture + conventional
vs conventional alone: RR 1.12 (95% CI 1.03–1.23). Evidence certainty:
low to very low. No sham controls.

Dedicated MA (Qiao et al. 2020, 11 RCTs, 1,718 participants):
bloodletting at EX-HN6 + eye drops vs eye drops alone. Effectiveness
RR 1.13 (95% CI 1.08–1.18 after sensitivity analysis, I²=0%). No
adverse reactions reported. Limitations: poor methodological quality,
all Chinese studies, publication bias identified.

Dose-response RCT (Qiao et al. 2020, n=108, 3 arms): 100 μL group
had lower VAS pain scores than 50 μL group, both superior to
medication-only. Quantified dose-response for bloodletting volume.

**② Hypertension — 🟡 Plausible**

Systematic review (Kwon et al. 2018, 8 RCTs, 543 participants):
immediate SBP-lowering effect significant at 5 min to 1 hour
post-intervention. DBP reduction not significant. Long-term benefits
demonstrated when combined with antihypertensive drugs. No sham
controls.

**③ Acute stroke — 🔴 Unvalidated**

MA (Kim & Han 2024, 6 RCTs, 530 participants): NIHSS score reduced
by 2.83 points, GCS improved by 2.75 points, hospital stay shortened
by 7.39 days. All Chinese, 2016–2023. High risk of bias.

**④ Fever — 🔴 Unvalidated**

SR (2025, European Journal of Integrative Medicine): adjuvant
bloodletting may improve antipyretic rates in adults. Very low
certainty. No advantage shown in children.

#### Dynamic acupoint properties

The ear apex becomes hyperemic (reddened, warm, engorged) during
febrile states and after auricular massage. This vascular engorgement
is the basis for the pre-procedure massage — designed to increase
local blood flow and facilitate blood expression. Whether measurable
changes in nerve sensitivity occur at the ear apex during pathological
states is undocumented. The "dynamic" aspect is more vascular
(engorgement) than neural (sensitization).
→ Cross-ref: C04 S4 (Dynamic Acupoint Phenomenon)

#### Cơ chế giải thích (BSA framework attribution)

**Bloodletting mechanism — multiple hypotheses, none confirmed:**

1. **Nociceptive counter-irritation (DNIC):** pricking produces acute
   nociceptive input via V3 and C2-C3 afferents → descending pain
   inhibition (diffuse noxious inhibitory controls). May explain acute
   headache relief. V3 auriculotemporal afferents project to trigeminal
   brainstem nuclei, accessing the same pain-modulation circuits as
   other head points.

2. **Autonomic reflex:** V3 auriculotemporal nerve carries
   postganglionic parasympathetic fibers from the otic ganglion.
   Nociceptive stimulation may reflexively modulate autonomic tone,
   potentially explaining the acute SBP-lowering effect
   (parasympathetic activation → ↓heart rate → ↓BP). The C2-C3 great
   auricular contribution adds a cervical autonomic component.

3. **Local microcirculatory effect:** bloodletting reduces local venous
   congestion. For hordeolum, the mechanism may be partly local and
   partly systemic (nociceptive-autonomic reflex).

**ABVN exclusion:** the ear apex is NOT in ABVN territory.
Anti-inflammatory and autonomic effects cannot be attributed to vagal
afferent stimulation — distinguishing Erjian from conchal auricular
points where ABVN-mediated vagal activation IS the proposed mechanism.

Gap: no specific molecular effector identified. Acute BP reduction
mechanism is the most clinically interesting unsolved question.
→ Cross-ref: A01 S1.4, A02 S3.5

#### Kỹ thuật kích thích + dose-response

Thích huyết (bloodletting, primary technique):
1. Massage auricle until red and warm (increases local circulation)
2. Sterilize ear apex with 75% alcohol
3. Fold ear anteriorly, locate highest point of auricular crease
4. Prick with three-edged needle (三棱针) or disposable lancet
5. Depth: 1–2 mm (one study used up to 4 mm)
6. Blood volume: 3–10 drops (quantified: 50–100 μL)
7. Frequency: once daily for 3–5 consecutive days (acute conditions)
8. Hemostasis: cotton pressure

Dose-response: 100 μL (~6 drops) > 50 μL (~3 drops) for stye pain
(Qiao et al. 2020, n=108).

Châm (B01): standard needling is NOT the primary application. If
used: 0.1–0.2 cun transverse along the helix rim. Not commonly
retained.

Bấm huyệt (B02): ear apex pressure/pinch for headache occasionally
described but not standard.

Cứu (B08): not applicable (cartilage burn risk).

Dry needling (B11): not applicable (no muscle tissue).
→ Cross-ref: B01 S3.1

#### An toàn

**Bleeding disorders:** anticoagulated patients and those with
bleeding disorders should NOT receive ear apex bloodletting.

**Infection risk:** ear cartilage has limited blood supply —
perichondritis from infection is difficult to treat and can cause
permanent auricular deformity (cauliflower ear). Strict aseptic
technique essential. Use disposable sterile lancets.

**Cartilage damage:** excessive depth (>2 mm) risks cartilage
penetration. Stay superficial — target is dermal capillaries.

**Volume control:** express only 3–10 drops. Excessive bloodletting
increases infection/hematoma risk.

**Pregnancy:** NOT contraindicated, though caution with any
bloodletting procedure during pregnancy is appropriate.

---

### EX-HN7 — Cầu Hậu (球后, Qiúhòu)

**Evidence tier: B (Standard Entry)**

"Behind the Globe" (球 = globe/eyeball, 后 = behind/posterior). At the
junction of the lateral 1/4 and medial 3/4 of the inferior orbital
margin — a specialized point accessing the RETROBULBAR space behind
the eyeball. One of the MOST DANGEROUS acupoints in the entire
361+48 system. The needle passes between the eyeball and the orbital
floor to reach retrobulbar fat and structures including the inferior
oblique muscle, posterior ciliary artery, ophthalmic artery, and optic
nerve. Used exclusively for serious ophthalmic conditions: optic
atrophy, glaucoma, retinitis pigmentosa. Comprehensive safety review
(Lee et al. 2023) documented globe perforation in 80% of serious
adverse event cases — injuries occurred even with licensed
practitioners. Comparable in danger to CV22 (tracheal puncture) and
GV15/GV16 (cisterna magna → brainstem).

#### Vị trí giải phẫu

WHO standard location (WPRO 2008): on the face, at the junction of
the lateral one-quarter and medial three-quarters of the infraorbital
margin.

Anatomical landmarks: palpate the inferior orbital rim from lateral
(near GB1 Tongziliao) to medial (near BL1 Jingming). The point at
3/4 of the way from lateral to medial lies approximately below the
pupil center, slightly lateral. The orbital floor curves posteriorly,
creating the path for needle insertion into the retrobulbar space.

Needle depth: 0.5–1.0 cun (13–25 mm) perpendicular, following the
contour of the inferior orbital wall. Cadaver study data: safe zone
extends to ~13 mm; posterior ciliary artery at 26.5 ± 1.7 mm; optic
nerve/ophthalmic artery at ~40 mm; superior ophthalmic vein at
~41 mm. **Recommended maximum: 26 mm.**

#### Cấu trúc mô học tại điểm

Nerve supply:
- **Infraorbital nerve (V2 maxillary division):** exits the
  infraorbital foramen, supplies lower eyelid, cheek, lateral nose.
  The needle passes BELOW the infraorbital nerve to enter the orbit.
- **Cranial nerve II (optic nerve):** at ~40 mm depth — the MOST
  CRITICAL structure to avoid.
- **Cranial nerves III (oculomotor), IV (trochlear), VI (abducens):**
  traverse the orbital apex near the optic nerve. Damage →
  extraocular muscle paralysis.

Spinal segments: none (cranial nerve territory).

Tissue layers traversed (cadaver study, vertical insertion): skin →
subcutaneous tissue → orbicularis oculi muscle → orbital septum →
orbital fat body → inferior oblique muscle → posterior orbital fat →
periorbital structures.

Vascular (in order of depth):
- Superficial: angular artery (terminal branch of facial artery)
- ~26.5 mm: **posterior ciliary arteries** (supply choroid and optic
  nerve head — damage → potential retinal ischemia)
- ~40 mm: **ophthalmic artery** (branch of internal carotid) + central
  retinal artery
- ~41 mm: superior ophthalmic vein
→ Cross-ref: A01 S1.4

#### Phân loại huyệt (Point Category Membership)

Extra point (经外奇穴): EX-HN7 in WHO nomenclature. Not on any
meridian.

Five Shu: not applicable. Yuan-Source: not applicable.
Luo-Connecting: not applicable. Xi-Cleft: not applicable.
Back-Shu: not applicable. Front-Mu: not applicable.
Eight Confluent: not applicable. Hui-Meeting: not applicable.

Special designations:
- **Specialized ophthalmic point:** one of 5 periorbital points with
  orbital/retrobulbar access: BL1 (superomedial), ST1 (infraorbital),
  EX-HN7 (inferomedial-lateral), GB1 (lateral), TE23 (limited access).
- **BL1+EX-HN7 pair:** Jingming and Qiuhou used together as the
  "periorbital orbital-access pair" — BL1 from superomedial, EX-HN7
  from inferolateral, bracketing the globe.
- **DANGEROUS point:** comparable to CV22, GV15/GV16. Restricted to
  practitioners with ophthalmic acupuncture training.
→ Cross-ref: AP-07 BL1 (retrobulbar safety), AP-03 ST1 (orbital
safety), AP-11 GB1

#### Cross-tradition mapping

**1. TCM acupoint:** EX-HN7 (Qiuhou 球后, "Behind the Globe").
Functions: benefits the eyes (利目), brightens vision (明目). Primary
indications: optic atrophy, optic neuritis, retinitis pigmentosa,
glaucoma, myopia, strabismus. The NARROWEST indication range of any
Tier B point — exclusively ophthalmic. ✓
