*Phase 2 Batch 7 COMPLETE. 9 entries added: EX-CA1 (Zigong, Tier B,
4/8 convergence — "Palace of the Child," sole EX-CA category point,
functional "Front-Mu of the Uterus," L1 ilioinguinal/iliohypogastric
at caudal boundary of uterine viscerotome T10-L1, round ligament
proximity provides direct anatomical uterus connection, Zhenjiu
Dacheng 1601 CE infertility primary indication, perimenopausal EA
RCT n=58 comparable to HRT, infrared radiation menstrual-cycle-
dependent acupoint specificity PMC6699375, pregnancy
CONTRAINDICATED), EX-UE1 (Zhoujian, Tier C, 1/8 — olecranon
moxibustion for scrofula, ulnar nerve proximity), EX-UE2 (Erbai,
Tier C, 1/8 — forearm distal treatment for hemorrhoids), EX-UE3
(Zhongquan, Tier C, 1/8 — dorsal wrist, chest/gastric pain), EX-UE4
(Zhongkui, Tier C, 1/8 — middle finger PIP, nausea/hiccup moxa),
EX-UE5 (Dagukong, Tier C, 1/8 — thumb IP, eye disorders), EX-UE6
(Xiaogukong, Tier C, 1/8 — little finger PIP, eye/throat), EX-UE7
(Yaotongdian, Tier B, 2/8 — premier distal point for acute lumbar
sprain, movement acupuncture 动气针法 exemplar, originally Weiling +
Jingling from Gong Tingxian 1604 pediatric tuina manual, SR/MA
protocol published PMC7850650 unfulfilled, controlled study n=180
faster onset not significant vs tuina, NON-SEGMENTAL mechanism C8-T1
→ L1-S1 via supraspinal descending modulation, "dynamically
activated" vs "dynamically sensitized" paradigm distinction), EX-UE8
(Wailaogong/Luozhen, Tier C, 2/8 — dorsal hand opposite PC8, stiff
neck/torticollis). **EX-CA REGION 1/1 COMPLETE. EX-UE 8/11 (UE1-UE8
complete, remaining UE9-UE11 in Batch 8).** Total entries in AP-15:
32/48 (including Phase 1 Yintang). No new ⚡ framework notes (running
total: 41). Key findings: (1) EX-CA1 provides DUAL mechanism for
uterine access — segmental (L1 viscerosomatic convergence at uterine
viscerotome boundary) + local (round ligament anatomical connection
continuous with myometrium); (2) EX-UE7 represents the clearest
challenge to segmental acupuncture theory in the extra points — hand
C8-T1 treating lumbar L1-S1 with zero segmental overlap, requiring
supraspinal descending modulation; (3) the 动气针法 "dynamically
activated" paradigm (EX-UE7, EX-UE8) is conceptually distinct from
the "dynamically sensitized" paradigm (Dingchuan, Dannangxue) — both
are dynamic acupoint expressions but through different mechanisms;
(4) the upper-extremity Tier C points reveal a pattern of DISTAL
treatment empiricism — forearm for hemorrhoids (EX-UE2), finger
joints for eye disorders (EX-UE5/6), hand for neck (EX-UE8) — all
lacking cross-tradition convergence and segmental logic, suggesting
a distinct category of TCM-only empirical observations.*

### EX-UE9 — Bát Tà (八邪, Bāxié)

**Evidence tier: B (Standard Entry)**

"Eight Pathogens" (八 = eight + 邪 = pathogen/evil). A group of 8
points (4 per hand) at the proximal web margins between the fingers,
on the dorsum of the hand. The 邪 (xié) in the name refers to the
classical concept that pathogenic factors enter the body through the
interstices between the fingers — the web spaces are viewed as gates
through which external pathogens (风 wind, 寒 cold, 湿 damp, 热 heat)
penetrate the hand and upper limb. Also known as 八鬼 (Bā Guǐ, "Eight
Ghosts") in some classical sources — NOT related to Sun Si-miao's 13
Ghost Points (鬼穴), which represent a separate psychiatric designation.
The hand-foot analog pair: Baxie (hand webs) ↔ EX-LE10 Bafeng
(八风, "Eight Winds," foot webs) — extra points recognize hand-foot
homology more explicitly than the meridian system.

#### Lịch sử và danh pháp (History and Nomenclature)

The concept of 八邪 appears in the Huangdi Neijing (Suwen "Ci Nue
Lun" — "for all malarial diseases when the pulse cannot be detected,
prick between the ten fingers to draw blood"; Lingshu Chapter 17/23
in the "59 piercings" for heat diseases) — but these classical
references describe pathogenic CONCEPTS and unnamed needling sites
rather than formalized acupoints. The web space points were first
formally named "八邪" in the **Yi Jing Xiao Xue** (《医经小学》, Liu
Chun 刘纯, **1388 CE**, Ming Dynasty). The **Zhenjiu Dacheng**
(《针灸大成》, Yang Jizhou, 1601 CE) later provided detailed
documentation with individual names for each web space:
- **大都 (Dàdū, "Great Capital"):** 1st web space (thumb–index)
- **上都 (Shàngdū, "Upper Capital"):** 2nd web space (index–middle)
- **中都 (Zhōngdū, "Central Capital"):** 3rd web space (middle–ring)
- **下都 (Xiàdū, "Lower Capital"):** 4th web space (ring–little)

Note: 大都 and 中都 are naming collisions with SP2 Dadu and LR6
Zhongdu — different points, same characters. Context (extra point
vs meridian point) disambiguates.

The modern WHO-WPRO system uses only the collective name Baxie
(EX-UE9) without individual point names.

The alternative name 八鬼 (Bā Guǐ, "Eight Ghosts") appears in some
compilations. The 鬼 (ghost) here refers to the invisible nature of
pathogenic wind/cold entering through the web spaces — a metaphorical
usage distinct from the 13 Ghost Points (鬼穴) used for psychiatric
conditions.

Hán-Việt: Bát Tà (八邪). Bát = eight, Tà = pathogen/evil.

#### Vị trí giải phẫu

WHO standard location (WPRO 2008): on the dorsum of both hands, at
the junction of the web between each pair of fingers, proximal to
the web margins. 4 points per hand, 8 total.

Individual point positions:
- **Point 1:** 1st web space (thumb–index finger), at the proximal
  web margin
- **Point 2:** 2nd web space (index–middle finger), at the proximal
  web margin
- **Point 3:** 3rd web space (middle–ring finger), at the proximal
  web margin
- **Point 4:** 4th web space (ring–little finger), at the proximal
  web margin

Spatial relationships:
- **LI4 (Hegu):** in the 1st web space but at the MIDPOINT of the
  2nd metacarpal bone — significantly more proximal than Baxie Point
  1. LI4 sits in the belly of the 1st dorsal interosseous muscle;
  Baxie Point 1 is at the web fold itself, ~2-3 cm distal.
- **EX-UE7 (Yaotongdian):** also between metacarpals but at the
  metacarpal MIDPOINTS (motor points of dorsal interossei) — more
  proximal than Baxie's web margin position.
- **EX-LE10 (Bafeng):** anatomical equivalent on the foot — 4 points
  per foot at toe web margins.

Needle depth: 0.5–0.8 cun (12–20 mm) perpendicular or slightly
oblique toward the palm, between the metacarpal shafts. At the
actual web margin, tissue depth is limited; deeper insertions enter
the interosseous space between the metacarpals.

#### Cấu trúc mô học tại điểm

Nerve supply varies by web space:
- **Point 1 (1st web space):** dorsal digital nerves from the
  superficial branch of the radial nerve (C6-C7). The 1st web space
  has the richest sensory innervation of any web space.
- **Point 2 (2nd web space):** dorsal digital nerves from the
  superficial branch of the radial nerve (C6-C7).
- **Point 3 (3rd web space):** the radial-ulnar transition zone.
  The dorsal digital nerve to the radial side of the ring finger may
  come from either the radial or ulnar nerve (anatomical variant).
- **Point 4 (4th web space):** dorsal branch of the ulnar nerve
  (C8-T1).
→ Cross-ref: A01 S2.3

Spinal segments: C6-C7 (radial side, Points 1-2), C8-T1 (ulnar
side, Point 4), variable for Point 3.

Motor innervation in the web spaces: the dorsal interosseous muscles
(4 per hand) occupy the spaces between metacarpals. The 1st dorsal
interosseous (1st web space) is innervated by the deep branch of the
ulnar nerve (C8-T1) — note the sensory-motor nerve mismatch: sensory
= radial nerve (C6-C7), motor = ulnar nerve (C8-T1). All 4 dorsal
interossei receive motor supply from the deep ulnar nerve.

Vascular: dorsal metacarpal arteries from the dorsal carpal arch.
Dorsal digital arteries at each web space. Rich venous network
(dorsal venous arch of the hand).

#### Phân loại huyệt (Point Category Membership)

Extra point (经外奇穴): EX-UE9 in WHO nomenclature. Not on any
meridian.

Five Shu: not applicable. Yuan-Source: not applicable.
Luo-Connecting: not applicable. Xi-Cleft: not applicable.
Back-Shu: not applicable. Front-Mu: not applicable.
Eight Confluent: not applicable. Hui-Meeting: not applicable.

Special designations:
- **Multi-point group (8 points):** 4 bilateral pairs treated as a
  functional unit. Clinical practice may use all 8 or select specific
  web spaces based on symptom location.
- **Hand-foot paired group:** Baxie (hand) ↔ Bafeng (foot) represent
  the most explicit hand-foot homology in the extra point system.
  Both access interdigital web spaces using identical positioning
  logic (dorsum, proximal web margin, 4 interspaces per extremity).
  This anatomical parallelism is more direct than the meridian
  system's hand-foot channel pairings (where LI ↔ ST is
  channel-based, not anatomically direct).
- **Web space access system:** the 4 web spaces provide sequential
  access to the radial-to-ulnar nerve gradient across the hand:
  C6 → C7 → C7/C8 → C8-T1.
→ Cross-ref: B01 S1.3.3

#### Cross-tradition mapping

**1. TCM acupoint:** EX-UE9 Baxie (八邪, "Eight Pathogens").
Functions: clears heat (清热), dissipates swelling (消肿), dispels
wind (祛风), opens the channels (通经络). Primary indications:
finger/hand pain, numbness, swelling, stiffness, spasm and
contracture of the fingers. Secondary: headache, toothache, sore
throat, fever, malaria (historical). ✓

**2. Trigger point:** the 4 dorsal interosseous muscles develop
trigger points at their motor point regions — between the metacarpal
shafts, slightly proximal to the Baxie web margin position. The 1st
dorsal interosseous trigger point (overlapping LI4 territory) is the
best-documented, with referred pain to the dorsal 1st web space and
index finger. Trigger points in the 2nd-4th dorsal interossei refer
pain locally to the hand and fingers (Travell & Simons). Baxie points
are distal to the classical trigger point motor point locations but
within the same interosseous territory. 🟡

**3. Ashi correspondence:** the web spaces are commonly tender in
hand conditions: rheumatoid arthritis (MCP joint inflammation
radiates proximally), hand edema (fluid accumulates in loose web
tissue), and generalized hand pain. Web space palpation tenderness
is a common clinical finding in inflammatory hand conditions. 🟡

**4. Marma point:** Kshipra Marma (क्षिप्र) — at the 1st web space
between thumb and index finger. Classified as Sira (vascular) Marma
in the Ayurvedic 107-point system. Anatomically corresponds to
Baxie Point 1 / LI4 territory. Kshipra exists on BOTH hand (1st
web space) and foot (between hallux and 2nd toe = LR3 territory).
Only the 1st web space has a Marma correspondence; web spaces 2-4
have no equivalent Marma. Partial match for the group. 🟡
→ Cross-ref: AP-02 LI4, AP-12 LR3

**5-8:** No correspondence for Head zone (C6-T1 hand dermatomes,
no viscerotome — the non-local indications like headache and sore
throat cannot be explained by segmental convergence), Anatomy Trains
(arm line terminations but not a specific convergence node at web
spaces), Neurogenic (no Kim 2017 data for hand web spaces), or
Cupping (not applicable at web spaces). ✗

**Convergence score: 4/8** (TCM ✓, Trigger point 🟡, Ashi 🟡,
Marma 🟡). MODERATE-HIGH. The 1st web space (Baxie Point 1)
individually would score higher due to the LI4/Kshipra/1st dorsal
interosseous trigger point triple convergence; web spaces 2-4 have
lower individual convergence. The group score reflects averaging
across all 4 positions.

#### Chỉ định truyền thống (DATA — ghi nhận, không endorse as mechanism)

Nguồn cổ:
- Zhenjiu Dacheng: hand/finger pain, numbness, swelling, spasm
  and contracture, redness and heat of the hand
- Classical broader indications: headache, toothache, sore throat,
  fever, malaria (疟疾). The malaria indication reflects the 邪
  (pathogen) concept — web spaces as pathogenic entry points.

Nguồn hiện đại (Deadman, WHO):
- Primary: pain, numbness, swelling, stiffness of fingers and hand
- Secondary: headache, toothache, sore throat
- Used in rheumatoid arthritis protocols for hand involvement
- Post-stroke hand rehabilitation (finger spasticity, contracture)

Kinh nghiệm lâm sàng nổi bật:
- **Baxie + LI4 (Hegu):** combined for comprehensive 1st web space
  coverage — LI4 at the motor point, Baxie Point 1 at the web margin
- **Baxie bilateral:** all 8 points for generalized hand conditions
  (rheumatoid arthritis, edema, neuropathy)
- **Baxie (hand) + Bafeng (foot):** upper + lower extremity
  simultaneous treatment for systemic conditions (fever, peripheral
  neuropathy, beriberi historical)

#### Bằng chứng hiện đại

No systematic review or RCT specifically isolating Baxie as the sole
intervention. Baxie appears in multi-point protocols for:

**① Post-stroke hand dysfunction — 🟡 Plausible (combination)**
A systematic review of acupuncture therapies for hand dysfunction in
post-stroke patients (PMC12139417, 2025) includes protocols using web
space points for finger spasticity and motor recovery. Baxie is used
as part of combination protocols (typically with LI4, TE5, LI11,
and local hand points), not as a sole intervention.

**② Rheumatoid arthritis hand involvement — 🟡 Plausible (combination)**
Widely used in clinical practice for rheumatoid arthritis hand
symptoms (pain, stiffness, swelling of MCP and PIP joints). No
point-specific controlled evidence for Baxie alone.

**③ Peripheral neuropathy — 🔴 Unvalidated**
Traditional indication with no modern controlled evidence specific
to Baxie.

**Overall assessment:** Baxie lacks point-specific evidence — always
used in combination protocols. Its clinical value is in providing
DIRECT ACCESS to the interdigital spaces for local hand conditions
rather than producing distal or systemic effects.

#### Cơ chế giải thích

**Local hand conditions (segmental):**
Needling at the web margins → dorsal digital nerve afferents
(C6-T1) → dorsal horn convergence at corresponding cervical
segments → segmental modulation of hand/finger pain, inflammation,
and motor function. This is a straightforward SEGMENTAL mechanism
— the treatment site and the treatment target are in the same
dermatome. Gate control theory applies directly: web space Aβ/Aδ
stimulation inhibits C-fiber pain transmission from the same hand
territory.

**Non-local indications (non-segmental):**
The traditional indications for headache, toothache, and sore throat
via hand web space stimulation share the same explanatory gap as LI4
treating facial pain — hand C6-T1 territory has NO segmental overlap
with trigeminal V1-V3 (face) or C2-C4 (throat). Mechanism requires
supraspinal descending modulation (PAG → rostroventromedial medulla),
the same pathway proposed for LI4's non-segmental effects.
→ Cross-ref: AP-02 LI4 mechanism, C04 S3.3

**Confidence:** 🟡 Synthesis for local effects (segmental mechanism
plausible). 🔴 Hypothesis for non-local effects (non-segmental
mechanism not confirmed for Baxie specifically).

#### Kỹ thuật kích thích

**Needling:** perpendicular or slightly oblique toward the palm,
0.5–0.8 cun (12–20 mm). The needle passes between the metacarpal
shafts through the interosseous space. De Qi typically described as
distension and aching in the web space, sometimes radiating to the
adjacent fingers.

**Pricking/bloodletting:** the web margins are a traditional
bloodletting site — prick with a three-edged needle (三棱针) and
express several drops of blood. Used for acute conditions: fever,
sore throat, hand swelling with heat. The rich dorsal venous network
provides readily accessible superficial veins.

**Moxibustion:** applicable but less common than needling. Direct
moxa (3-5 cones) or moxa stick at the web spaces.

**Selection within the group:** clinical practice selects web spaces
based on the affected fingers. All 8 points needled simultaneously
for generalized hand conditions. Single web space for localized
finger problems. Bilateral (both hands) for systemic indications.

#### Dynamic acupoint properties

No specific dynamic acupoint data for Baxie. The web spaces do not
show documented disease-state sensitization or electrical property
changes in published literature. In inflammatory hand conditions
(rheumatoid arthritis, gout), web space tenderness increases —
consistent with local tissue inflammation rather than the
organ-specific dynamic acupoint phenomenon described for Dingchuan
(impedance) or Dannangxue (tenderness = gallbladder pathology).

#### An toàn

**Low risk overall.** The web spaces provide relatively safe needling
access to the interosseous space.

**Digital nerve/vessel:** the dorsal digital nerves and arteries run
along the sides of the fingers, passing through the web spaces.
Needle insertion at the web margin center (equidistant from both
bordering digital neurovascular bundles) minimizes contact risk.
Bruising from dorsal venous network puncture is common but benign.

**Depth limitation:** deeper insertion (>1 cun) may reach the palmar
interosseous space. Standard depth (0.5-0.8 cun) is safe.

**1st web space caution:** Baxie Point 1 should be distinguished from
LI4 — different depth, angle, and clinical intent. Deep needling
toward the palm at this point approaches the adductor pollicis and
the deep palmar arch.

**Pregnancy:** NOT specifically contraindicated (unlike LI4 which is
traditionally contraindicated in pregnancy — Baxie Point 1, being at
the web margin rather than the LI4 motor point, does not carry the
same LI4 pregnancy restriction).

---

### EX-UE10 — Tứ Phùng (四缝, Sìfèng)

**Evidence tier: B (Standard Entry)**

"Four Seams" (四 = four + 缝 = seam/crease). 4 points at the midpoint
of the PIP (proximal interphalangeal) joint transverse creases of the
index through little fingers (digits 2-5), on the PALMAR surface.
The thumb is excluded (it has only an IP joint, no PIP). Arguably the
most unique acupoint in the entire 361+48 system: the ONLY point
with a primarily PEDIATRIC indication (childhood malnutrition 疳积
Gan Ji), a UNIQUE technique (pricking to express fluid rather than
standard acupuncture needling), and a DIAGNOSTIC feature (the
presence or absence of yellowish viscous fluid at the puncture site
correlates with the child's nutritional status). Sifeng challenges
the conventional acupoint paradigm — it functions more as a
tissue-diagnostic and micro-intervention site than as a traditional
needling target.

#### Lịch sử và danh pháp (History and Nomenclature)

Sifeng first documented in the **Qi Xiao Liang Fang** (《奇效良方》,
"Remarkable Effective Formulas," compiled by Dong Su 董宿, **1470 CE**,
Ming Dynasty Chenghua period). Volume 55 of this 70-volume text
covers acupuncture and is notably the first text to establish a
dedicated section for "extraordinary points" (奇穴) as a distinct
category. The Zhenjiu Dacheng (1601 CE) expanded the clinical
description, specifying that in mild cases pricking expresses blood
only, while in severe cases yellowish viscous fluid is squeezed out.
The association with childhood malnutrition (疳积, Gan Ji) is ancient
— Gan Ji was one of the most common and serious pediatric conditions
in historical China, characterized by malnutrition, abdominal
distension, emaciation, poor appetite, and failure to thrive. Before
modern nutritional interventions, Gan Ji had significant childhood
mortality.

The 缝 (fèng, "seam") refers to the PIP joint transverse crease —
the natural fold line on the palmar surface where the finger bends.
The 4 seams are the PIP creases of fingers 2-5.

The technique is specifically PEDIATRIC in origin. Classical texts
describe Sifeng pricking for children aged approximately 1-10 years.
Adult use developed later as a secondary extension, but the pediatric
application remains the primary and defining indication.

Hán-Việt: Tứ Phùng (四缝). Tứ = four, Phùng = seam/crease.

#### Vị trí giải phẫu

WHO standard location (WPRO 2008): on the palmar surface of digits
2-5, at the midpoint of the transverse crease of the proximal
interphalangeal (PIP) joint. 4 points per hand, 8 total bilateral.

Individual point positions:
- **Point 1:** index finger PIP crease center (palmar)
- **Point 2:** middle finger PIP crease center (palmar)
- **Point 3:** ring finger PIP crease center (palmar)
- **Point 4:** little finger PIP crease center (palmar)

The thumb is excluded because it has only one interphalangeal joint
(IP, not PIP) — and the single-joint thumb IP crease is not part of
the Sifeng group.

Depth: the palmar digital skin at the PIP crease is thin (1-2 mm
skin + subcutaneous tissue) with the flexor digitorum superficialis
(FDS) tendon sheath immediately deep. The PIP joint capsule is
deeper (~5-8 mm from skin surface). Pricking depth is superficial
(1-3 mm) — sufficient to reach subcutaneous tissue without entering
the tendon sheath or joint capsule.

#### Cấu trúc mô học tại điểm

Nerve supply:
- **Points 1-2 (index, middle finger):** proper palmar digital
  nerves from the median nerve (C7-C8). The palmar digital nerves
  run along the sides of each finger, providing extremely rich
  sensory innervation of the fingertip pads and palmar surfaces.
- **Point 3 (ring finger):** dual innervation — the radial side from
  the median nerve, the ulnar side from the ulnar nerve (C8-T1).
  The ring finger PIP is the median-ulnar transition zone.
- **Point 4 (little finger):** proper palmar digital nerve from the
  ulnar nerve (C8-T1).
→ Cross-ref: A01 S2.3

Spinal segments: C7-C8 (median nerve territory, Points 1-3 radial
side), C8-T1 (ulnar nerve territory, Points 3 ulnar side-4).

Structures deep to the pricking site (superficial → deep):
- Skin (thin palmar digital skin, ~0.5-1 mm at creases)
- Subcutaneous tissue (minimal fat at PIP creases)
- Flexor digitorum superficialis (FDS) tendon within its synovial
  sheath — the FDS tendon splits at this level to allow passage of
  the flexor digitorum profundus (FDP) tendon
- A2 pulley (annular pulley, critical for finger flexion mechanics)
- PIP joint capsule (deeper, ~5-8 mm)

Vascular: proper palmar digital arteries run alongside the digital
nerves on each side of the finger. At the PIP crease, transverse
digital arteries create a local vascular arch.

#### Phân loại huyệt (Point Category Membership)

Extra point (经外奇穴): EX-UE10 in WHO nomenclature. Not on any
meridian.

Five Shu: not applicable. Yuan-Source: not applicable.
Luo-Connecting: not applicable. Xi-Cleft: not applicable.
Back-Shu: not applicable. Front-Mu: not applicable.
Eight Confluent: not applicable. Hui-Meeting: not applicable.

Special designations:
- **Only pediatric-specific acupoint:** Sifeng is arguably the ONLY
  point in the 361+48 standardized acupoint system with a primary
  pediatric indication. While other points are used in children (HT9
  for febrile seizures, GV26 for loss of consciousness), their
  indications apply to all ages. Sifeng's primary indication —
  childhood malnutrition/Gan Ji — is age-specific by definition.
- **Pricking-only technique:** unlike all other acupoints (which
  primarily use retention needling, moxibustion, or acupressure),
  Sifeng uses a PRICKING technique: rapid puncture → express fluid →
  withdraw. No needle retention. This technique is unique in the
  standardized acupoint repertoire.
- **Diagnostic-therapeutic dual function:** the fluid expressed from
  Sifeng has diagnostic significance — yellowish viscous fluid in
  malnutrition vs only blood in healthy children. The point serves
  BOTH diagnostic (identifying malnutrition status) and therapeutic
  (treating the condition) functions simultaneously.
→ Cross-ref: B01 S1.3.3

#### Cross-tradition mapping

**1. TCM acupoint:** EX-UE10 Sifeng (四缝, "Four Seams"). Functions:
strengthens the Spleen (健脾), eliminates accumulation (消积), clears
heat (清热), removes stagnation (消食导滞). Primary indication:
childhood malnutrition/Gan Ji (疳积), pediatric anorexia, failure to
thrive. Secondary: childhood digestive disorders, whooping cough
(百日咳), intestinal parasites (蛔虫症). ✓

**2-8:** No correspondence found in any of the 7 non-TCM traditions.
The PIP joint palmar creases are not recognized as clinically
significant sites in any non-TCM healing tradition:
- Trigger point: no skeletal muscle at PIP crease (tendons only). ✗
- Ashi: PIP creases are not commonly tender on palpation (unless
  inflammatory arthritis). ✗
- Marma: no Marma point at finger joint creases. ✗
- Head zone: C7-T1 hand dermatome has no viscerotome correspondence
  for digestive organs (stomach/spleen/intestine = T6-T12). ✗
- Anatomy Trains: no fascial convergence at PIP creases. ✗
- Neurogenic: no Kim 2017 data for finger surfaces. ✗
- Cupping: physically impossible at finger creases. ✗

**Convergence score: 1/8** (TCM ✓). LOW — the lowest possible
convergence. Sifeng represents a PURELY TCM empirical observation
with zero independent validation from any other healing tradition.
This extreme low convergence, combined with the strongest
point-specific evidence of any 1/8-scoring point (24-RCT SR/MA),
creates a paradox: robust clinical data for a location that no other
tradition independently identified. The clinical observation is REAL;
the explanatory gap is MAXIMAL.

#### Chỉ định truyền thống (DATA — ghi nhận, không endorse as mechanism)

Nguồn cổ:
- Primary: **疳积 (Gan Ji)** — childhood malnutrition syndrome.
  Classical description: a child with abdominal distension,
  emaciation, sallow complexion, poor appetite, abnormal stools,
  and irritability. Modern correlates: protein-energy malnutrition,
  failure to thrive, pediatric anorexia.
- Secondary: childhood anorexia, poor appetite, indigestion
- Additional: whooping cough (百日咳), intestinal parasites

Nguồn hiện đại (Deadman, WHO):
- Childhood malnutrition, pediatric anorexia
- Indigestion, abdominal distension in children
- Some sources extend to adult digestive disorders (secondary
  development, not the defining indication)

The yellowish fluid phenomenon:
- In children with Gan Ji: pricking Sifeng expresses a yellowish,
  viscous, translucent fluid (sometimes described as serous or
  lymphatic-like)
- In healthy children (or after successful treatment): pricking
  expresses only clear blood, no viscous fluid
- The fluid's presence/absence serves as a DIAGNOSTIC indicator of
  the child's nutritional-digestive status
- No definitive modern laboratory analysis of this fluid's
  composition has been published. Clinical descriptions suggest
  interstitial/subcutaneous tissue fluid with altered composition
  due to nutritional deficiency — NOT synovial fluid (PIP joint
  capsule is deeper than the pricking depth)

#### Bằng chứng hiện đại

**① Pediatric anorexia — 🟡 Plausible (point-specific SR/MA)**

**Systematic review and meta-analysis:** Sun et al. 2023
(Complementary Therapies in Medicine, PMID 37748564) — "Acupuncture
at Sifeng (EX-UE 10) for pediatric anorexia." **24 RCTs, n=2,202
children.** One of the rare POINT-SPECIFIC systematic reviews for an
extra point (most SRs evaluate combination protocols).

Key findings:
- Low certainty evidence suggests Sifeng pricking is effective and
  safe for pediatric anorexia
- 3 studies showed increased body weight after treatment
- No serious adverse effects reported across all 24 studies — no
  localized erythema, ulceration, or fever; no abnormalities in
  micronutrient or blood tests
- Quality limitations: high or unclear risk of bias in allocation
  concealment, blinding, and selective reporting

Notable: evaluates Sifeng as the SOLE intervention (not in
combination). Sample size (n=2,202) is substantial for acupuncture
research. Zero adverse effects across 24 studies is reassuring for
a pediatric invasive technique.
🟡 Plausible (positive point-specific SR but low certainty)

**② Antibiotic-associated diarrhea in infants — 🟡 Plausible**

Sifeng pricking combined with tuina has been studied for antibiotic-
associated diarrhea in infants. Limited evidence but consistent with
the traditional digestive indication extended to modern pediatric
gastroenterology.

**③ Adult digestive disorders — 🔴 Unvalidated**

Extension to adult use lacks controlled evidence.

#### Cơ chế giải thích

**The mechanism is UNKNOWN.** Sifeng presents one of the deepest
explanatory gaps in the entire acupoint system:

1. **No segmental logic:** the finger PIP creases (C7-T1 dermatome)
   have ZERO viscerotome overlap with the digestive organs (stomach
   T6-T9, spleen T6-T10, small intestine T9-T11, large intestine
   T11-L1). No segmental convergence pathway exists between the
   treatment site and the therapeutic target.

2. **No established non-segmental framework:** unlike EX-UE7
   Yaotongdian (where supraspinal descending modulation provides a
   plausible non-segmental mechanism for C8-T1→L1-S1), the connection
   between finger PIP pricking and childhood digestive improvement
   has no established neuroanatomical pathway.

3. **Possible hypotheses** (all speculative):
   - **Local tissue fluid drainage:** the pricking may drain
     pathological fluid accumulation at the PIP flexion creases,
     providing a local tissue intervention rather than a neural
     reflex. The yellowish fluid's disappearance after treatment
     suggests a tissue-level rather than neural mechanism.
   - **Nociceptive-systemic activation:** intense nociceptive input
     from bilateral finger pricking (16 sites total) may produce
     systemic autonomic effects — sympathetic activation → gut
     motility modulation, similar to the general mechanism proposed
     for strong acupuncture stimulation.
   - **Immunomodulation via micro-injury:** breaking the skin barrier
     at 16 sites creates a controlled micro-inflammatory response
     that may have systemic immunomodulatory effects relevant to
     gut-associated lymphoid tissue function.

**Confidence:** 🔴 Hypothesis. No confirmed mechanism. The clinical
observation (24 RCTs positive) significantly OUTPACES the mechanistic
understanding — the reverse of the usual pattern where mechanism is
proposed before clinical validation.

#### Kỹ thuật kích thích

**THREE-EDGED NEEDLE PRICKING (三棱针点刺):** the defining technique.
NOT standard filiform needle retention.

Procedure:
1. **Preparation:** clean the PIP creases of all 4 fingers (digits
   2-5) bilaterally with antiseptic. Total: 8 pricking sites.
2. **Instrument:** three-edged needle (三棱针, sānléng zhēn) or
   sterile lancet. The triangular cross-section creates a puncture
   that allows fluid expression.
3. **Technique:** hold the finger firmly. Prick quickly at the
   center of the PIP crease, 1-3 mm depth (subcutaneous tissue only
   — do NOT penetrate the tendon sheath or joint capsule). Immediate
   withdrawal.
4. **Expression:** squeeze the fingertip-to-PIP area gently.
   In malnutrition: yellowish viscous fluid emerges, followed by
   blood. Express until only blood appears. In health: only clear
   blood, no viscous fluid.
5. **Post-treatment:** clean, apply pressure for hemostasis.
6. **Frequency:** typically 1-2 sessions per week, 3-5 sessions per
   course. May repeat after 1-2 week intervals.

**NOT used with:**
- Filiform needle retention (standard acupuncture)
- Moxibustion (inappropriate at palmar PIP creases)
- Electroacupuncture (not applicable to pricking technique)

**Age considerations:** classically for children aged 1-10 years.
In infants <1 year, the technique is modified with shallower pricking
and minimal expression.

#### Dynamic acupoint properties

**The yellowish fluid = a dynamic tissue marker:**
The fluid expressed at Sifeng in malnutrition but absent in health
represents a form of dynamic tissue response — the point's clinical
significance (fluid presence) is contingent on the patient's
pathological state. This parallels the Dynamic Acupoint Phenomenon
(C04 §4) but through tissue fluid rather than neural sensitization:

- **Dannangxue (EX-LE6):** tenderness = gallbladder pathology
  (NEURAL sensitization)
- **Dingchuan (EX-B1):** impedance change = asthma (ELECTRICAL
  property change)
- **Sifeng (EX-UE10):** fluid expression = malnutrition (TISSUE
  FLUID change)

All three represent disease-state-dependent acupoint changes, but
through different biological channels. Sifeng's dynamic response is
unique in the Atlas — no other acupoint demonstrates a detectable
FLUID change correlated with pathological status.
→ Cross-ref: C04 S4

#### An toàn

**Moderate risk** (higher than typical acupoints due to pediatric
population and skin-breaking technique).

**Infection:** the primary safety concern. Multiple skin punctures in
young children require strict aseptic technique. Use single-use
sterile lancets or three-edged needles. Clean each site before and
after pricking.

**Tendon sheath penetration:** the flexor digitorum superficialis
tendon sheath lies immediately deep to the subcutaneous tissue at
the PIP crease. Excessive depth (>3 mm) risks penetrating the sheath,
potentially introducing infection (infectious tenosynovitis =
surgical emergency). CRITICAL: maintain superficial depth (1-3 mm).

**PIP joint capsule:** the joint capsule is 5-8 mm deep — well below
the standard pricking depth. Inadvertent joint penetration is
unlikely at proper technique but would risk septic arthritis.

**Digital nerve damage:** the proper palmar digital nerves run along
the SIDES of the fingers, not through the crease CENTER. Central PIP
crease pricking avoids the neurovascular bundles. Laterally deviated
pricking risks digital nerve contact.

**Bleeding:** the 24-study SR/MA reported no significant bleeding
complications. Gentle pressure achieves hemostasis rapidly.

**Age considerations:** extreme caution in infants <6 months (thin
skin, immature hemostasis). Some practitioners recommend minimum age
of 6-12 months.

**Pregnancy:** NOT applicable (pediatric-specific technique).

---

### EX-UE11 — Thập Tuyên (十宣, Shíxuān)

**Evidence tier: B (Standard Entry)**

"Ten Proclamations" or "Ten Diffusions" (十 = ten + 宣 = proclaim/
diffuse). 10 points at the center of each fingertip, one per finger
(both hands). Located 0.1 cun (~2 mm) from the free edge of the nail
— at the FINGERTIP PAD CENTER, not the nail corner. This position
distinguishes Shixuan from the 12 standard Jing-Well points (which
are at the nail corners): the meridian system places emergency points
where channels begin/end (nail corners), while Shixuan places them
where sensory receptor density is MAXIMAL (fingertip pad center). An
emergency bloodletting point group: pricking all 10 fingertips for
loss of consciousness, stroke, febrile seizures, and heatstroke. The
hand-foot analog pair: Shixuan (10 fingertips) ↔ EX-LE12 Qiduan
(气端, "Qi End," 10 toe tips).

#### Lịch sử và danh pháp (History and Nomenclature)

The technique predates the name. The earliest reference to fingertip
emergency stimulation appears in **Beiji Qianjin Yaofang** (Sun
Si-miao, **652 CE**): "灸手十指爪下各三壮" — "moxa the area under
the nails of all ten fingers, three cones each," for sudden collapse
(卒忤死). This describes the same anatomical location but as unnamed
emergency MOXIBUSTION, not bloodletting. The formal name "十宣" and
classification as a distinct extra point first appears in the **Qi
Xiao Liang Fang** (Dong Su, **1470 CE**), the same text that first
named Sifeng and systematized extra point classification.

The name 十宣 (Shixuan): 十 = ten (all fingers), 宣 = to proclaim,
to diffuse, to disseminate. The name suggests that pricking "opens"
or "diffuses" blocked Qi at all 10 extremity tips simultaneously —
a classical TCM interpretation of emergency arousal through intense
peripheral stimulation.

The relationship with Jing-Well points is historically significant:
the Lingshu describes Jing-Well points as "where Qi emerges" (井者
所出为井) and associates them with emergency revival and transitions
between consciousness and unconsciousness. Shixuan EXTENDS this
concept from the meridian-specific nail corners to the anatomically
optimal fingertip centers — a shift from channel theory to sensory
anatomy.
→ Cross-ref: AP-05 HT9 (L1961, 2008), AP-09 PC9 (L2087, 2141)

Hán-Việt: Thập Tuyên (十宣). Thập = ten, Tuyên = proclaim/diffuse.

#### Vị trí giải phẫu

WHO standard location (WPRO 2008): on the tips of the 10 fingers,
0.1 cun (~2 mm) distal to the free edge of the fingernail, at the
center of the fingertip pad. 10 points total (5 per hand).

**Distinction from Jing-Well points:**

| Feature | Jing-Well (12 points) | Shixuan (10 points) |
|---------|----------------------|---------------------|
| Position | Nail CORNER (radial or ulnar border) | Fingertip PAD CENTER |
| Identity | Meridian-specific (LU11, LI1, PC9, TE1, HT9, SI1) | Extra point group (no individual identity) |
| Fingers | 6 per hand (one per meridian) | ALL 5 per hand |
| Classification | Five Shu: 1st Shu ("where Qi emerges") | Extra point, no Five Shu |
| Distance apart | ~3-5 mm between Jing-Well at nail corner and Shixuan at pad center on the same finger |

Spatial relationships:
- Each fingertip has BOTH a Jing-Well point (at the nail corner) AND
  a Shixuan point (at the pad center) — these are DISTINCT locations
  separated by ~3-5 mm
- EX-LE12 Qiduan: equivalent toe tip points

Depth: superficial pricking only — 1-2 mm, sufficient to reach the
capillary bed for bloodletting.

#### Cấu trúc mô học tại điểm

The fingertips contain one of the HIGHEST sensory receptor densities
in the entire body:

Nerve supply (all 10 fingertips):
- **Thumb, index, middle, radial ring:** proper palmar digital nerves
  from the median nerve (C6-C8)
- **Ulnar ring, little finger:** proper palmar digital nerves from
  the ulnar nerve (C8-T1)
- Dorsal contribution from dorsal digital nerves (radial and ulnar)
  extending over the fingertip dorsum
→ Cross-ref: A01 S2.3

Spinal segments: C6-C8 (median territory), C8-T1 (ulnar territory).

Receptor density:
- **Meissner corpuscles:** highest concentration in the body —
  ~140/cm² in fingertip skin (vs ~25/cm² in palm). Fast-adapting
  mechanoreceptors for fine touch discrimination.
- **Merkel cell-neurite complexes:** highest concentration in the
  body. Slowly adapting type I mechanoreceptors. Two-point
  discrimination threshold: ~2 mm at fingertips — the finest in the
  body (vs ~40 mm on the back).
- **Free nerve endings (nociceptors):** extremely dense in fingertip
  dermis. Both Aδ (sharp pain) and C fibers (dull pain). Fingertip
  pricking produces one of the most INTENSE nociceptive signals per
  unit area of any body site.

Vascular: proper palmar digital arteries terminate in a rich
capillary network at the fingertip — the basis for the bloodletting
technique. Arteriovenous anastomoses (Sucquet-Hoyer canals) regulate
fingertip blood flow and respond to sympathetic activation.

#### Phân loại huyệt (Point Category Membership)

Extra point (经外奇穴): EX-UE11 in WHO nomenclature. Not on any
meridian.

Five Shu: not applicable. Yuan-Source: not applicable.
Luo-Connecting: not applicable. Xi-Cleft: not applicable.
Back-Shu: not applicable. Front-Mu: not applicable.
Eight Confluent: not applicable. Hui-Meeting: not applicable.

Special designations:
- **Emergency bloodletting group:** Shixuan is almost exclusively
  used in acute/emergency situations. Unlike most acupoints which
  have chronic and acute indications, Shixuan's value is concentrated
  in emergency arousal and acute fever management.
- **Jing-Well extension:** functionally extends the Jing-Well
  emergency concept from channel-specific nail corners to the
  anatomically optimal fingertip pad center. The shift from nail
  corner to pad center moves from CHANNEL THEORY (endpoints of
  meridians) to SENSORY ANATOMY (maximum receptor density).
- **Multi-point group (10 points):** all 10 pricked simultaneously
  or in rapid sequence. The collective stimulation of all fingertips
  creates a bilateral, multi-segmental (C6-T1) nociceptive barrage.
- **Hand-foot paired group:** Shixuan (10 fingertips) ↔ EX-LE12
  Qiduan (10 toe tips).
→ Cross-ref: B01 S3.1 (Jing-Well points)

#### Cross-tradition mapping

**1. TCM acupoint:** EX-UE11 Shixuan (十宣, "Ten Proclamations/
Diffusions"). Functions: opens the orifices (开窍), clears heat
(清热), restores consciousness (苏厥), revives from wind-stroke
(醒脑). Primary indications: loss of consciousness, coma, wind-stroke
(中风), febrile convulsions, heatstroke. Secondary: sore throat,
infantile convulsions, fingertip numbness. ✓

**2. Trigger point:** no correspondence. Fingertip pads have no
skeletal muscle — digital pulp consists of fibroadipose tissue
compartmentalized by fibrous septae connecting skin to periosteum. ✗

**3. Ashi correspondence:** fingertips are tender in Raynaud's
phenomenon, peripheral neuropathy, and digital ischemia. In patients
with impaired consciousness (the primary Shixuan indication),
fingertip sensitivity may be diagnostically altered. 🟡

**4-8:** No correspondence for Marma (no fingertip Marma), Head zone
(C6-T1, no viscerotome — emergency arousal mechanism is non-segmental
by definition), Anatomy Trains (arm line terminations, not clinically
significant), Neurogenic (no Kim 2017 fingertip data), or Cupping
(not applicable at fingertips). ✗

**Convergence score: 2/8** (TCM ✓, Ashi 🟡). LOW. Consistent with
other fingertip points in the Atlas: HT9 (2/8), PC9 (1/8). Fingertip
points are a TCM-specific therapeutic location — no other healing
tradition independently identified the fingertips as emergency
treatment sites.

#### Chỉ định truyền thống (DATA — ghi nhận, không endorse as mechanism)

Nguồn cổ:
- Classical emergency acupuncture: "for sudden loss of consciousness,
  wind-stroke, and epilepsy, prick the ten fingertips and release
  several drops of blood"
- Zhenjiu Jiayi Jing (259 CE, Huangfu Mi): references fingertip
  pricking for consciousness disorders (at Jing-Well points; Shixuan
  extends this concept)
- Zhenjiu Dacheng (1601): formalizes Shixuan as a 10-point emergency
  group

Nguồn hiện đại (Deadman, WHO):
- Loss of consciousness, coma, wind-stroke (stroke)
- Heatstroke, febrile seizures in children
- Epileptic seizures (emergency)
- Sore throat, tonsillitis
- Fingertip numbness

Kinh nghiệm lâm sàng nổi bật:
- **Shixuan + GV26 (Renzhong) + PC9 (Zhongchong) + KI1 (Yongquan):**
  the classic consciousness-restoration protocol. GV26 (philtrum,
  trigeminal V2), Shixuan/PC9 (fingertips, C6-T1), KI1 (sole, S1) —
  multi-level intense nociceptive input from face, hands, and feet
  simultaneously.
→ Cross-ref: AP-05 HT9 (L2047), AP-09 PC9 (L2112)

#### Bằng chứng hiện đại

**① Acute stroke (adjunctive bloodletting) — 🟡 Plausible**

Systematic review and meta-analysis: Zhang et al. 2024 (Healthcare,
PMC11507497) — "Bloodletting for Acute Stroke Recovery." 17 RCTs.

Key findings:
- Bloodletting group showed significant improvement in neurological
  deficits: NIHSS mean difference −2.08 (95% CI: −3.13 to −1.02)
- Treatment effective rate improved: RR 1.17 (95% CI: 1.11 to 1.22)
- Upper extremity motor function (Fugl-Meyer Assessment): MD 12.20
  (95% CI: 9.67 to 14.73)
- Lower extremity: MD 3.86 (95% CI: 2.16 to 5.56)

Limitations:
- The SR includes multiple bloodletting sites (Jing-Well points,
  Shixuan, ear apex EX-HN6, other sites) — Shixuan's individual
  contribution is NOT isolated
- Small sample sizes, low quality evidence (GRADE: low to very low)
- Bloodletting used as ADJUNCTIVE to conventional Western stroke
  treatment, not as sole intervention
🟡 Plausible (positive adjunctive effect, but Shixuan not isolated)

**② Febrile seizures in children — 🔴 Unvalidated**

Traditional indication with no controlled modern evidence specific to
Shixuan. The nociceptive arousal mechanism is plausible but untested.

**③ Heatstroke — 🔴 Unvalidated**

Emergency indication documented in classical texts. No modern
controlled studies.

**Overall assessment:** Shixuan bloodletting has emerging evidence as
adjunctive therapy for acute stroke recovery, but point-specific
evidence isolating Shixuan from other bloodletting sites does not
exist. The strongest argument for Shixuan is anatomical: fingertip
pad centers have the highest sensory receptor density in the body,
making them the biomechanically optimal site for nociceptive arousal.

#### Cơ chế giải thích

**Nociceptive arousal pathway:**
Fingertip pricking → intense Aδ nociceptive input from the highest
receptor density site in the body → ascending spinothalamic and
spinoreticular pathways (from cervical dorsal horn C6-T1) → brainstem
reticular activating system (RAS) → cortical arousal → consciousness
restoration.

This mechanism is NON-SPECIFIC — it does not require channel theory,
meridian pathways, or point-specific effects. ANY intense nociceptive
stimulus can produce arousal, and the fingertips are the optimal site
because:
1. **Maximum receptor density:** ~140 Meissner corpuscles/cm², dense
   free nerve endings
2. **Disproportionate cortical representation:** fingertips occupy a
   disproportionately large area of the somatosensory cortex (sensory
   homunculus) — pricking 10 fingertips activates a massive cortical
   area relative to the physical surface area stimulated
3. **Bilateral, multi-segmental input:** 10 fingertips across C6-T1
   create a multi-segment bilateral nociceptive barrage, maximizing
   reticular formation activation
4. **Sympathetic co-activation:** intense nociception → sympathetic
   nervous system activation → increased heart rate, blood pressure,
   cerebral blood flow — all contributing to arousal

The HT9 and PC9 entries in this Atlas noted the same non-specific
arousal mechanism. Shixuan's advantage over individual Jing-Well
points is SCALE: 10 simultaneous nociceptive inputs vs 1-2.

**Confidence:** 🟡 Synthesis. The nociceptive arousal pathway is
well-established neuroscience. The specific ACUPOINT contribution
(beyond simple nociception) is undemonstrated — any intense fingertip
stimulus would likely produce similar effects regardless of exact
point location.
→ Cross-ref: AP-05 HT9, AP-09 PC9

#### Kỹ thuật kích thích

**PRICKING/BLOODLETTING (点刺放血):**

Procedure:
1. **Preparation:** clean all 10 fingertips with antiseptic. Have
   gauze/cotton ready for hemostasis.
2. **Instrument:** three-edged needle (三棱针), sterile lancet, or
   26-gauge hypodermic needle.
3. **Technique:** hold the finger firmly, squeeze the fingertip
   distally to engorge the capillary bed. Prick quickly at the center
   of the fingertip pad, 1-2 mm depth. Immediate withdrawal.
4. **Blood expression:** squeeze the fingertip to express 3-5 drops
   of blood from each fingertip. Blood should flow freely (indicating
   adequate capillary access).
5. **Sequence:** typically all 10 fingertips in rapid succession
   (emergency: speed matters). Start with both thumbs or middle
   fingers and proceed through all digits.
6. **Post-treatment:** clean, apply pressure for hemostasis.

**In emergency settings:** the entire procedure (10 fingertips) can
be completed in 2-3 minutes — one of the fastest acupuncture
interventions. Requires minimal equipment (a sterile needle or
lancet). This simplicity and speed contribute to Shixuan's historical
value as a first-aid technique.

**Needle-free alternatives:**
- Strong fingertip pressure/pinching (emergency first aid without
  needles — same nociceptive arousal mechanism, reduced intensity)
- Fingertip tapping/slapping in pediatric emergencies

#### Dynamic acupoint properties

Shixuan does NOT demonstrate classic dynamic acupoint properties.
Unlike Dannangxue (tenderness appears with gallbladder pathology) or
Dingchuan (impedance changes with asthma), Shixuan fingertip points
are ALWAYS anatomically present and maximally innervated regardless
of disease state. The fingertips do not "sensitize" or "activate" in
response to pathology — their therapeutic value derives from inherent
extreme sensory density, not from disease-dependent changes.

This distinguishes Shixuan from the Dynamic Acupoint Phenomenon
(C04 §4): Shixuan's effectiveness is LOCATION-BASED (maximum receptor
density) rather than STATE-DEPENDENT (pathological sensitization).

#### An toàn

**Low risk.** One of the safest acupuncture interventions due to
superficial depth and brief contact time.

**Infection:** standard aseptic technique. Use single-use sterile
instruments. Clean each fingertip before and after pricking.

**Bleeding:** minimal blood loss (3-5 drops per fingertip × 10 ≈
0.5-1 mL total). Negligible in hemodynamically stable patients.
**Caution in coagulopathy:** patients on anticoagulants or with
bleeding disorders may have prolonged bleeding — apply sustained
pressure.

**Pain:** fingertip pricking produces SHARP, INTENSE but brief pain
(Aδ fiber activation). In conscious patients, this is the primary
discomfort. In unconscious patients (the primary indication), pain
perception is reduced — the nociceptive arousal mechanism works BELOW
the level of conscious pain perception.

**Pediatric considerations:** fingertip pricking in children for
febrile seizures requires careful restraint and rapid technique.

**Pregnancy:** NOT specifically contraindicated.

---

*Phase 2 Batch 8 COMPLETE. 3 entries added: EX-UE9 (Baxie, Tier B,
4/8 convergence — "Eight Pathogens" at 4 finger web spaces per hand,
Kshipra Marma at 1st web, dorsal interosseous trigger point zone,
hand-foot paired with Bafeng, local segmental + non-segmental for
headache/throat same gap as LI4), EX-UE10 (Sifeng, Tier B, 1/8
convergence — "Four Seams" at PIP creases palmar, ONLY
pediatric-specific acupoint in the 361+48 system, unique pricking
technique for childhood malnutrition/Gan Ji, SR/MA 24 RCTs n=2,202
for pediatric anorexia PMID 37748564, yellowish fluid = tissue-
diagnostic dynamic response, mechanism UNKNOWN = maximal explanatory
gap with zero segmental logic C7-T1→digestive organs), EX-UE11
(Shixuan, Tier B, 2/8 convergence — "Ten Diffusions" at 10 fingertip
pad centers, emergency bloodletting, extends Jing-Well from nail
corners to pad centers shifting channel theory → sensory anatomy,
stroke SR/MA 17 RCTs NIHSS −2.08 adjunctive PMC11507497,
nociceptive arousal via maximum receptor density = non-specific
mechanism). **EX-UE REGION 11/11 COMPLETE.** Total entries in AP-15:
35/48 (including Phase 1 Yintang). No new ⚡ framework notes (running
total: 41). Key findings: (1) Sifeng's 1/8 convergence + 24-RCT
SR/MA creates the maximum paradox of clinical evidence without
cross-tradition validation or mechanistic explanation — the opposite
of EX-HN5 Taiyang (7/8 convergence, limited point-specific evidence);
(2) Shixuan demonstrates the conceptual shift from CHANNEL THEORY
(Jing-Well at nail corners where meridians begin/end) to SENSORY
ANATOMY (Shixuan at fingertip pad centers with maximum Meissner/
Merkel receptor density); (3) Sifeng's yellowish fluid represents a
third modality of dynamic acupoint response — tissue fluid change
alongside neural sensitization (Dannangxue) and electrical property
change (Dingchuan); (4) the Batch 8 trio represents three distinct
multi-point paradigms — web-space access for local conditions (Baxie,
segmental), tissue-diagnostic pricking for pediatric malnutrition
(Sifeng, unknown mechanism), and nociceptive arousal for emergency
revival (Shixuan, non-specific mechanism).*
