### EX-LE6 — Đởm Nang (胆囊穴, Dǎnnáng Xué)

**Evidence tier: B (Standard Entry)**

"Gallbladder Point" (胆囊 = gallbladder + 穴 = acupoint) — a MODERN
diagnostic extra point with a purely descriptive name, unlike the
metaphorical naming conventions of classical acupoints. Dannangxue
emerged from clinical observation during the 1950s-1960s in Chinese
hospitals, making it one of the few acupoints identified in the 20th
century rather than classical antiquity. Its primary clinical value is
DIAGNOSTIC: tenderness at this point during palpation indicates
gallbladder pathology (cholecystitis, cholelithiasis). Located 1-2 cun
below GB34 (Yanglingquan) on the RIGHT leg, at the tender point on the
anterior border of the fibula. Dannangxue is the PREMIER example of a
Dynamic Acupoint — clinically silent in healthy individuals, becoming
significant only under pathological conditions. Presents a striking
SEGMENTAL MISMATCH: gallbladder viscerotome (T6-T9) has no direct
overlap with the point's somatotome (L4-S2).

#### Lịch sử và danh pháp (History and Nomenclature)

Dannangxue does NOT appear in any classical acupuncture text. Unlike
virtually all other points in the 361+48 WHO system, which trace their
origins to texts spanning from the Huangdi Neijing (c. 100 BCE) through
the Zhenjiu Dacheng (1601 CE), Dannangxue is a product of MODERN
clinical observation. It was identified during the systematic clinical
documentation of diagnostic acupoint phenomena in the People's Republic
of China during the 1950s-1960s, when large-scale hospital practice
generated empirical observations about point tenderness correlating with
specific organ pathologies.

The name is purely anatomical-descriptive: 胆囊 (dǎnnáng) = gallbladder
(anatomical organ name), 穴 (xué) = acupoint. This contrasts sharply
with the metaphorical naming tradition of classical acupoints (e.g.,
"Crane's Summit," "Hundred Bug Nest," "Solitary Yin"). The plain naming
reflects its modern, observation-based origin.

Formerly classified as M-LE-23 in older nomenclature systems. Currently
standardized as EX-LE6 in WHO-WPRO.

The parallel diagnostic point EX-LE7 Lanweixue (阑尾穴, "Appendix
Point") was identified through the same paradigm during the same era —
together, they represent a distinctly modern class of diagnostic extra
points.

Hán-Việt: Đởm Nang Huyệt (胆囊穴). Đởm = gallbladder, Nang = sac
(together = gallbladder organ).

#### Vị trí giải phẫu

WHO standard location (WPRO 2008): on the lateral aspect of the right
lower leg, at the tender point approximately 1-2 cun directly below
GB34 (Yanglingquan), on the anterior border of the fibula.

**LOCATION METHOD — PALPATION-BASED:** Unlike most acupoints with fixed
anatomical landmarks, Dannangxue is located by PALPATION for the most
tender point along the anterior fibular border, 1-2 cun below GB34.
The exact position varies between individuals and with disease state —
in acute cholecystitis, the tender zone may be broader and more easily
located; in chronic or subclinical disease, it may be subtle or absent.

**UNILATERAL:** One of very few acupoints that exist on only ONE side —
the RIGHT leg, corresponding to the anatomical position of the
gallbladder (right upper quadrant). Some sources note bilateral
tenderness may occur in severe cases.

Spatial relationships:
- **GB34 (Yanglingquan):** in the depression anterior and inferior to
  the head of the fibula. Dannangxue is 1-2 cun (~2-4 cm) directly
  below GB34 along the anterior fibular border. GB34 is the He-Sea of
  Gallbladder AND the Hui-Meeting of Sinews — Dannangxue lies in its
  immediate zone of influence.
- **ST36 (Zusanli):** approximately 2 cun medial, on the tibial side.
  Different nerve territory (deep peroneal vs superficial peroneal).
- **EX-LE7 (Lanweixue):** approximately 2 cun below ST36, on the
  anterior tibial side — the appendix diagnostic point. Dannangxue and
  Lanweixue are on DIFFERENT sides of the leg (lateral fibular vs
  anterior tibial) and in different nerve territories.

Needle depth: 1.0-1.5 cun (25-38 mm) perpendicular. At the fibular
border, deeper insertion enters the anterior compartment between the
fibula and the peroneus longus/extensor digitorum longus muscles.

#### Cấu trúc mô học tại điểm

Nerve supply:
- **Lateral cutaneous nerve of the calf (L5-S2):** cutaneous supply to
  the lateral leg below the knee. A branch of the common peroneal nerve.
- **Superficial peroneal nerve (L4-S1):** continues along the anterior
  fibular border in the lateral compartment — Dannangxue sits in its
  territory.
- **Common peroneal nerve (L4-S2):** the parent trunk passes around the
  fibular neck ~2-4 cm above Dannangxue.
- Spinal segments: L4-S2 (predominantly L5-S1).
→ Cross-ref: A01 S2

Vascular: branches of the anterior tibial artery (anterior compartment)
and peroneal artery (lateral compartment). Anterior tibial recurrent
artery branches in the proximal region.

Tissue layers (superficial → deep):
- Skin → subcutaneous tissue
- Peroneus longus muscle (or its tendinous portion at this level)
- Anterior compartment fascia → extensor digitorum longus
- Fibular periosteum (lateral)

#### Phân loại huyệt (Point Category Membership)

Extra point (经外奇穴): EX-LE6 in WHO nomenclature. Not on any
meridian.

Five Shu: not applicable. Yuan-Source: not applicable.
Luo-Connecting: not applicable. Xi-Cleft: not applicable.
Back-Shu: not applicable. Front-Mu: not applicable.
Eight Confluent: not applicable. Hui-Meeting: not applicable.

Special designations:
- **Diagnostic extra point:** primary value is DIAGNOSTIC — tenderness
  at this point during palpation is a clinical sign indicating
  gallbladder pathology.
- **Dynamic Acupoint par excellence:** the purest expression of the
  dynamic acupoint concept (C04 §4). Clinically silent in healthy
  individuals; becomes significant only under pathological conditions.
→ Cross-ref: C04 S4, AP-13 GV9 (L1351, 1395)

#### Cross-tradition mapping

**1. TCM acupoint:** EX-LE6 Dannangxue. Functions: benefits the
gallbladder, clears Damp-Heat, relieves pain (利胆，清湿热，止痛). ✓

**2. Trigger point:** peroneus longus has well-documented TrPs in the
proximal lateral leg, with referred pain to the lateral ankle. The TrPs
are musculoskeletal (not biliary-related) but share the anatomical
location. 🟡

**3. Ashi point:** Dannangxue IS fundamentally an Ashi point — it is
located by palpation for tenderness. Its clinical identity is "the
tender point below GB34 in gallbladder disease." 🟡

**4. Marma point:** no Marma correspondence in this region. ✗

**5. Head zone:** gallbladder Head zone = T7-T9 dermatome (epigastric,
right upper quadrant, infrascapular). Dannangxue sits in L5-S1
dermatome — OUTSIDE the gallbladder Head zone. This is a significant
MISMATCH: the Head zone model does NOT predict clinical significance at
L5 for gallbladder pathology. ✗

**6. Anatomy Trains:** Lateral Line (Myers) runs through the peroneus
longus, which passes through Dannangxue's territory at the lateral
fibular border. 🟡

**7. Neurogenic inflammatory spot:** Kim 2017 (PMID 29123119)
demonstrated that visceral disease produces neurogenic spots outside
strict dermatome boundaries — colitis-related spots appeared at L2-S2
and L4-L5, well outside the colonic viscerotome. The same non-segmental
mechanism could explain Dannangxue sensitization during gallbladder
pathology. 🟡

**8. Cupping point:** not a cupping location. ✗

**Convergence score: 5/8** (TCM ✓, Trigger point 🟡, Ashi 🟡,
Anatomy Trains 🟡, Neurogenic inflammatory spot 🟡). HIGH — despite
the Head zone mismatch, four independent traditions identify clinically
significant structures at or near this location.

#### Chỉ định truyền thống (DATA — ghi nhận, không endorse as mechanism)

- Primary: acute and chronic cholecystitis, cholelithiasis (gallstones),
  biliary colic
- Secondary: biliary ascariasis, jaundice, hypochondriac pain
- **DIAGNOSTIC function:** tenderness upon palpation at Dannangxue
  during physical examination is interpreted as a diagnostic sign for
  gallbladder pathology — analogous to Murphy's sign in Western medicine,
  though the anatomical rationale differs fundamentally.

#### Bằng chứng hiện đại

**Diagnostic accuracy:** No formal sensitivity/specificity study has
been published specifically testing Dannangxue tenderness for
gallbladder disease. The closest parallel comes from studies on EX-LE7
Lanweixue for appendicitis, which showed poor diagnostic performance
(sensitivity 22-64%, specificity 23-50%). No direct comparison between
Murphy's sign and Dannangxue tenderness has been conducted.

**Therapeutic evidence (specific to Dannangxue):** The evidence base
is thin — no published RCT isolates Dannangxue alone against credible
controls.

- **Song 2006** (Zhongguo Zhen Jiu): electroacupuncture for
  cholelithiasis using a 7-point protocol including Dannangxue. 86.7%
  effectiveness vs 68.3% comparison group. Multi-point protocol —
  Dannangxue's individual contribution cannot be isolated.
- **Sun et al. 2021** (PMID 34127059, Trials): protocol for a parallel
  fMRI RCT comparing GB34 vs EX-LE6 vs non-acupoint control, n=90.
  The ONLY indexed study designed to directly compare GB34 with
  Dannangxue. **Results remain UNPUBLISHED.**

**GB34 evidence (the adjacent channel point with much stronger data):**
- **Lee et al. 2001** (PMID 11174298, Gastrointest Endosc): n=11,
  sphincter of Oddi manometry during ERCP. EA at GB34 significantly
  decreased basal pressure, amplitude, frequency, and duration of SO
  phasic wave contractions (p<0.05). Effects reversible.
- **Zhao et al. 2017** (PMID 29070912, Sci Rep): guinea pig model,
  n=40. GB34 produced BIDIRECTIONAL biliary regulation — decreased SO
  activity while increasing gallbladder pressure (promoting emptying).
  Atropine abolished the effect, confirming cholinergic/parasympathetic
  mechanism.
- **Zhang et al. 2021** (Acupunct Electrother Res): n=60 chronic
  cholecystitis patients. EA at GB34 reduced gallbladder volume from
  49.6±5.66 ml to 23.0±2.83 ml after 30 min (p<0.01).

The evidence gap between Dannangxue (no therapeutic data) and GB34 (SO
manometry, bidirectional regulation, ultrasound-documented gallbladder
emptying) is striking — both points are within 2-4 cm of each other.

#### Cơ chế giải thích

**THE SEGMENTAL MISMATCH:** The central mechanistic puzzle. The
gallbladder's visceral afferents travel via the greater splanchnic
nerves to spinal segments T6-T9 (primarily T7-T8). Known referred pain
patterns from gallbladder disease project to: epigastric region (T7-T9
dermatome), right upper quadrant (T8-T10), right infrascapular
area/Boas sign (T7-T9 posterior), right shoulder/Kehr's sign (C3-C5
via phrenic nerve). ALL within segmentally expected territories.

Dannangxue sits at L5-S1 — there is NO segmental overlap between the
gallbladder viscerotome (T6-T9) and Dannangxue's somatotome (L4-S2).
Standard viscero-somatic convergence theory does not explain why
gallbladder pathology would produce tenderness here.

**Possible mechanisms for the non-segmental sensitization:**

**1. Non-segmental central sensitization:** Giamberardino et al. 2005
(PMID 15733650, Pain): 142 patients — gallbladder calculosis produces
referred hyperalgesia extending beyond strict dermatome boundaries.
Costantini et al. 2016 (PMID 27081848, PLoS One): 142 fibromyalgia
patients with gallbladder disease — visceral nociceptive input amplifies
hyperalgesia even at quadriceps (L3-L4), well below the gallbladder
viscerotome.

**2. Neurogenic inflammation beyond segmental limits:** Kim et al. 2017
(PMID 29123119, Sci Rep): visceral disease produces Evans Blue-positive
neurogenic inflammatory spots at acupoint locations (70% coincidence
with traditional acupoints). Colitis-related spots appeared at L2-S2
and L4-L5 — well outside the colonic viscerotome (T10-L1).

**3. Wide dynamic range neuron convergence:** Tan et al. 2019 (PMID
31485246): activation of WDR neurons and dorsal column nuclei during
acupoint sensitization could mediate extra-segmental referred phenomena
through supraspinal relay.

**4. Zone-of-influence hypothesis:** Dannangxue may represent tenderness
spreading from GB34 (which IS on the Gallbladder channel and has
demonstrated cholinergic biliary effects) into adjacent tissue. The
palpation-based location method ("find the tender point below GB34")
is essentially a search for the periphery of a sensitized zone.

The mechanism gap = HIGH. The diagnostic claim lacks both segmental
neuroanatomical explanation AND formal diagnostic accuracy validation.
→ Cross-ref: A07 S3 (dermatome-viscerotome), C04 S4 (dynamic acupoint)

#### Kỹ thuật kích thích

**Needling:** perpendicular insertion, 1.0-1.5 cun (25-38 mm). Achieve
De Qi with lift-thrust manipulation. Some protocols use reducing method
(泻法) for excess/Heat patterns.

**Electroacupuncture:** commonly used for biliary conditions. Paired
with GB34. Dense-disperse wave pattern.

**Moxibustion:** warming moxa applicable for Cold-Dampness patterns.
Less commonly used than needling or EA.

**Diagnostic palpation technique:** with the patient supine and knee
slightly flexed, apply gradual firm pressure along the anterior fibular
border from GB34 distally. The maximally tender point in a patient with
gallbladder pathology = Dannangxue. Compare right vs left leg —
asymmetric tenderness on the right is the key diagnostic finding.
→ Cross-ref: B01, B02

#### Dynamic acupoint properties

Dannangxue embodies the dynamic acupoint concept more purely than any
other point in the 361+48 system. It transitions through three states
(Tan 2019, PMID 31485246):

**Silent/Latent:** in healthy individuals, the point is clinically
non-significant — palpation along the anterior fibular border produces
no distinguishable tenderness.

**Passive (sensitized):** during active gallbladder pathology
(cholecystitis, biliary colic), the point becomes tender upon
palpation. Pressure pain threshold decreases. The sensitized zone
diameter may be <2 cm in chronic conditions.

**Active:** in acute cholecystitis or biliary colic, the point may be
spontaneously painful or painful with minimal contact. The sensitized
zone may expand (>2 cm).

**Measurable correlates of sensitization (general acupoint data, not
Dannangxue-specific):** elevated electrical conductance at sensitized
neurogenic spots (Kim 2017, F>12, p<0.001); decreased von Frey
withdrawal thresholds (Ben 2012, PMID 23227096); increased local
concentrations of substance P, CGRP, histamine, 5-HT; mast cell
aggregation correlating with disease severity; infrared temperature
elevation at sensitized cholecystitis-related acupoints (Tan 2019).

**EVIDENCE GAP:** No published study has specifically measured PPT,
electrical conductivity, or other biophysical parameters at Dannangxue
before, during, and after gallbladder disease. The dynamic acupoint
status is inferred from the diagnostic paradigm and from general
sensitization research at other acupoints.
→ Cross-ref: C04 S4

#### An toàn

**Low risk.** Adequate muscle cushion (peroneus longus, extensor
digitorum longus) over fibula at standard depth.

**Common peroneal nerve:** the parent trunk wraps around the fibular
neck ~2-4 cm ABOVE Dannangxue. At standard depth and location, direct
nerve trunk contact is unlikely. Redirect if sharp electrical pain
radiates to the dorsum of the foot.

**Periosteum:** the fibular shaft is close to the surface at the
anterolateral border. Periosteal contact may cause sharp localized
pain — redirect slightly.

**Pregnancy:** NOT specifically contraindicated.

---

### EX-LE7 — Lan Vĩ (阑尾穴, Lánwěi Xué)

**Evidence tier: B (Standard Entry)**

"Appendix Point" (阑尾 = appendix + 穴 = acupoint) — a MODERN
diagnostic extra point, identified through the same clinical observation
paradigm as EX-LE6 Dannangxue during the 1950s-1960s in Chinese
hospitals. Named after a Western anatomical structure — one of the very
few acupoints with a non-classical, descriptive name. Its primary value
was claimed to be DIAGNOSTIC: tenderness during palpation indicating
appendicitis. However, three prospective studies (Teubner 1988,
Alt-Epping 2002, Rezvani 2016) have shown POOR to MEDIOCRE diagnostic
performance (sensitivity 22-64%, specificity 23-50%), substantially
inferior to McBurney's sign. Located approximately 2 cun below ST36
(Zusanli) on the RIGHT leg, at the tender point. Shares the same
segmental mismatch puzzle as Dannangxue: appendix viscerotome (T10) has
no overlap with the point's somatotome (L4-L5). A 2018 review (PMID
30014668) concluded that Lanweixue is "not a point, but an area."

#### Lịch sử và danh pháp (History and Nomenclature)

Like Dannangxue, Lanweixue does NOT appear in any classical text. It is
a modern discovery (新订穴, xīndìng xué) from the mid-20th century,
identified during the era of Western-Chinese medicine integration in
post-1949 China.

The name is purely descriptive: 阑尾 (lánwěi) = appendix (Western
anatomical term adopted into Chinese), 穴 (xué) = acupoint. One of
very few acupoints named after a Western anatomical structure rather
than a metaphorical or classical Chinese concept.

**Classical precedent for appendicitis:** while Lanweixue is modern,
appendicitis was recognized in classical TCM as 肠痈 (cháng yōng,
"intestinal abscess"). Zhang Zhongjing's Jin Gui Yao Lue (c. 200 CE)
describes the condition and prescribes Da Huang Mu Dan Tang. The
classical management was herbal, not acupuncture-point-based.

A comprehensive review (PMID 30014668, Zhongguo Zhen Jiu, 2018)
concluded that Lanweixue represents "the product of Western and Chinese
combination" and is "not a point, but an area" — location determined
by palpation for tenderness rather than fixed anatomical landmarks.

**Nomenclature variants:** EX-LE7 (most common international), EX-LE8
(some Chinese textbooks), M-LE-13 (WHO/WPRO classification).

Hán-Việt: Lan Vĩ Huyệt (阑尾穴). Lan = barrier, Vĩ = tail (together =
appendix).

#### Vị trí giải phẫu

WHO standard location (WPRO 2008): on the anterior aspect of the right
lower leg, at the tender point approximately 2 cun below ST36 (Zusanli),
1 finger-breadth lateral to the anterior tibial crest.

**LOCATION METHOD — PALPATION-BASED:** Like Dannangxue, located by
palpation for the most tender point — "not a point, but an area"
(PMID 30014668).

**UNILATERAL:** Right leg only — corresponding to the appendix's
anatomical position in the right iliac fossa. One of very few
unilateral acupoints (paired with Dannangxue in this paradigm).

Spatial relationships:
- **ST36 (Zusanli):** 3 cun below ST35, 1 finger-breadth lateral to
  the anterior tibial crest. Lanweixue is ~2 cun distal — only ~5 cm
  apart in the SAME muscle (tibialis anterior), on the SAME nerve
  (deep peroneal). The anatomical similarity is striking.
- **ST37 (Shangjuxu):** 6 cun below ST35 — the Lower He-Sea of Large
  Intestine. Lanweixue falls BETWEEN ST36 and ST37 on the Stomach
  channel trajectory.
- **EX-LE6 (Dannangxue):** on the LATERAL fibular side, ~2 cun lateral
  distance. Different nerve territory (superficial peroneal for
  Dannangxue vs deep peroneal for Lanweixue).

**Lanweixue sits on the Stomach channel trajectory** (between ST36 and
ST37), though it is classified as an extra point rather than a channel
point.

Needle depth: 1.0-2.0 cun (25-50 mm) perpendicular, in the tibialis
anterior muscle mass.

#### Cấu trúc mô học tại điểm

Nerve supply:
- **Deep peroneal nerve (L4-L5):** the primary nerve of the anterior
  compartment, running between tibialis anterior and extensor digitorum
  longus. SAME nerve as ST36.
- **Lateral cutaneous nerve of the calf (L5-S1):** cutaneous
  innervation to the anterolateral leg surface.
- Spinal segments: L4-L5 (deep peroneal territory). Dermatome: L5.
→ Cross-ref: A01 S2

Vascular: anterior tibial artery — the main artery of the anterior
compartment, alongside the deep peroneal nerve. Same vascular supply
as ST36.

Tissue layers: skin → subcutaneous tissue → tibialis anterior muscle →
interosseous membrane → posterior compartment (at maximum depth).

#### Phân loại huyệt (Point Category Membership)

Extra point (经外奇穴): EX-LE7 in WHO nomenclature. Not on any
meridian (though anatomically on the Stomach channel trajectory).

Five Shu: not applicable. Yuan-Source: not applicable.
Luo-Connecting: not applicable. Xi-Cleft: not applicable.
Back-Shu: not applicable. Front-Mu: not applicable.
Eight Confluent: not applicable. Hui-Meeting: not applicable.

Special designations:
- **Diagnostic extra point:** paired with EX-LE6 Dannangxue as the
  second diagnostic point identified through modern clinical observation.
- **Dynamic acupoint:** same paradigm as Dannangxue — clinically
  significant only under pathological conditions.
→ Cross-ref: C04 S4

#### Cross-tradition mapping

**1. TCM acupoint:** EX-LE7 Lanweixue. Functions: clears Heat, resolves
toxins, regulates the intestines (清热解毒，调肠). ✓

**2. Trigger point:** tibialis anterior has well-documented TrPs in this
region, with referred pain to the dorsal foot and great toe. The TrPs
are musculoskeletal (not appendix-related) but share the location. 🟡

**3. Ashi point:** like Dannangxue, Lanweixue is fundamentally an Ashi
point — located by palpation for pathological tenderness. 🟡

**4. Marma point:** no Marma correspondence. ✗

**5. Head zone:** appendix Head zone = T10 dermatome (periumbilical,
right lower quadrant transition). Lanweixue sits in L5 dermatome —
OUTSIDE the appendix Head zone. Same segmental MISMATCH as Dannangxue
(organ T10 vs point L4-L5). ✗

**6. Anatomy Trains:** Superficial Front Line (SFL, Myers) runs through
the tibialis anterior at this level. 🟡

**7. Neurogenic inflammatory spot:** Kim 2017 (PMID 29123119) showed
non-segmental neurogenic spots from visceral disease at L4-L5 — the
model predicts possible sensitization at Lanweixue during appendiceal
inflammation. 🟡

**8. Cupping point:** not a cupping location. ✗

**Convergence score: 5/8** (TCM ✓, Trigger point 🟡, Ashi 🟡,
Anatomy Trains 🟡, Neurogenic inflammatory spot 🟡). HIGH — same
pattern as Dannangxue, reflecting the shared diagnostic paradigm.

#### Chỉ định truyền thống (DATA — ghi nhận, không endorse as mechanism)

- Primary: acute and chronic appendicitis (阑尾炎)
- Secondary: indigestion, lower abdominal pain, leg pain
- **DIAGNOSTIC function:** tenderness at Lanweixue during palpation was
  proposed as a diagnostic sign for appendicitis — analogous to
  McBurney's point in Western medicine.
- **Historical TCM equivalent:** 肠痈 (intestinal abscess), treated
  with Da Huang Mu Dan Tang since Zhang Zhongjing (c. 200 CE).

#### Bằng chứng hiện đại

**Diagnostic accuracy — THREE prospective studies, ALL negative or
mediocre:**

**1. Teubner 1988** (PMID 3380555, Pain): n=50 patients with
presumptive appendicitis, examined for Lanwei tenderness
pre-operatively. Conclusion: "statistically not a good indicator of
appendicitis."

**2. Alt-Epping et al. 2002** (PMID 12618551, Forsch Komplementarmed):
n=116 patients, prospective, pre-surgical. Sensitivity 64.4%,
specificity 50%, PPV 92.7%, NPV 12.5%, odds ratio 1.8. The Lanwei
sign ranked 7th-10th among 22 conventional clinical signs. Diagnostic
value "not sufficient" compared with McBurney's sign.

**3. Rezvani et al. 2016** (J Isfahan Med Sch): n=150 patients, two
Iranian hospitals. Sensitivity 22.6%, specificity 23.5%, accuracy
22.7% — essentially CHANCE-LEVEL performance. McBurney's sign in the
same study: sensitivity 95.5%, specificity 100%, accuracy 84.7%.

**Summary:** the diagnostic evidence does NOT support Lanweixue
tenderness as a reliable screening tool for appendicitis. The most
favorable study (Alt-Epping 2002) found high PPV (92.7%) but only in a
pre-selected surgical population.

**Therapeutic evidence:**

**Adib-Hajbaghery & Etri 2013** (PMID 24250696, J Res Med Sci):
single-blind RCT, n=70 post-appendectomy patients. Acupressure on
EX-LE7 (acupressure band device, 7 hours post-surgery). Pain VAS
decreased significantly in the intervention group (5.10→3.53, P=0.018)
vs control (4.45→4.01, P=0.32 n.s.). No effect on nausea/vomiting.

**Conservative treatment in China (1950s-1970s):** Chinese hospitals
used acupuncture combined with herbal medicine (Da Huang Mu Dan Tang
modifications) for uncomplicated acute appendicitis. Modern consensus:
appropriate ONLY for simple/uncomplicated appendicitis; complicated
cases (adhesion, purulent infection, perforation) require surgery
(PMID 30014668).

**Acupuncture anesthesia note:** during the acupuncture anesthesia era
(1960s-1970s), appendectomy protocols used bilateral ST36, ST37, and
ST39 — NOT Lanweixue specifically (PMID 1288930: n=80, ST36 acupuncture
vs epidural anesthesia, no significant difference in success rate).

#### Cơ chế giải thích

**THE SEGMENTAL MISMATCH — same puzzle as Dannangxue:** Appendix
visceral afferents travel via the lesser splanchnic nerve to spinal
segment T10. Known appendicitis referred pain follows classic McBurney
progression: periumbilical (T10 visceral) → right lower quadrant
(T10-T12 somatic/parietal peritoneum). ALL within T10-T12 territory.

Lanweixue sits at L4-L5 (deep peroneal nerve territory) — there is NO
segmental overlap with the appendix viscerotome (T10). Standard
viscero-somatic convergence theory does not explain why appendicitis
would produce tenderness at L4-L5.

**The same four mechanisms proposed for Dannangxue apply:**
1. Non-segmental central sensitization (Giamberardino 2005, PMID
   15733650; Costantini 2016, PMID 27081848)
2. Neurogenic inflammation beyond segmental limits (Kim 2017, PMID
   29123119)
3. WDR neuron convergence at supraspinal levels (Tan 2019, PMID
   31485246)
4. Zone-of-influence hypothesis (tenderness spreading from nearby
   channel points ST36/ST37)

**Additional consideration — evidence of non-existence:** The 2018
review (PMID 30014668) noted that Lanweixue is "not a point, but an
area." Combined with the poor diagnostic accuracy across three studies
(sensitivity 22-64%), the mechanism may not exist because the
phenomenon itself may be unreliable. If the sensitivity IS ~64% (per
Alt-Epping), the most likely explanation is non-segmental central
sensitization: not all patients develop extra-segmental spread, and
other conditions causing central sensitization could produce similar
tenderness (poor specificity).
→ Cross-ref: A07 S3, C04 S4

#### Kỹ thuật kích thích

**Needling:** perpendicular insertion, 1.0-2.0 cun (25-50 mm). Achieve
De Qi with lift-thrust manipulation. Strong reducing method (泻法)
for acute conditions.

**Warm needle moxibustion:** documented in clinical protocols — filiform
needles with moxa pieces attached to handles, 40-minute retention.

**Acupressure:** Adib-Hajbaghery 2013 (PMID 24250696) used acupressure
band device on EX-LE7 for post-appendectomy pain — 7 hours, loosened
10 min every 2 hours.

**Combined protocol:** typically not used in isolation — combined with
ST36, ST37 (Lower He-Sea of Large Intestine), ST25 (Front-Mu of Large
Intestine), CV12, and/or Ashi points, plus herbal medicine.
→ Cross-ref: B01, B02

#### Dynamic acupoint properties

Lanweixue shares the same dynamic acupoint paradigm as Dannangxue —
clinically silent in healthy individuals, becoming tender during
appendiceal pathology.

**However, the evidence BASE for this dynamic behavior is uniquely
NEGATIVE:** three prospective diagnostic studies (Teubner 1988,
Alt-Epping 2002, Rezvani 2016) collectively suggest that the
sensitivity of this dynamic phenomenon is POOR (22-64%). This means
the point FAILS to become adequately sensitized in 36-78% of
appendicitis patients.

**Possible interpretations:**
1. The dynamic sensitization occurs but is subtler than clinical
   palpation can reliably detect
2. The sensitization occurs only in certain disease stages or patient
   populations
3. The phenomenon is inconsistent or unreliable

**Comparison with other dynamic acupoints in the Atlas:**
- **Dannangxue** (EX-LE6, gallbladder): no formal diagnostic accuracy
  study → unknown sensitivity
- **Dingchuan** (EX-B1): skin impedance changes documented (Ngai 2009)
  → measurable dynamic with objective instrument
- **Heding** (EX-LE2): PPT reduction confirmed (Chen 2022, n=666) →
  measurable dynamic with algometry
- **Sifeng** (EX-UE10): yellowish fluid expression → visible dynamic

Lanweixue represents a case where CLAIMED dynamic behavior has been
specifically tested and found UNRELIABLE by Western diagnostic
standards. This does not invalidate the dynamic acupoint concept, but
demonstrates that not all claimed diagnostic sensitization phenomena
survive formal testing.
→ Cross-ref: C04 S4

#### An toàn

**Low risk at the needling site.** Standard anterior leg considerations.

**Deep peroneal nerve:** runs in the anterior compartment alongside the
anterior tibial artery. At standard depth in the tibialis anterior
muscle belly, the nerve is not typically contacted. Redirect if sharp
electrical pain radiates to the dorsal foot.

**Anterior tibial artery:** close companion of the deep peroneal nerve.
Deep perpendicular insertion could contact the artery — minor hematoma
risk.

**PRIMARY SAFETY CONCERN — DELAYED SURGERY:** The critical safety issue
is NOT the needling itself but the risk of using acupuncture/TCM to
DELAY necessary surgical intervention for appendicitis. Complicated
appendicitis (perforation, abscess, peritonitis) is a surgical
emergency. Warning signs requiring immediate surgical referral: sudden
severe pain, high fever (>38.3°C), rigid board-like abdomen, sudden
disappearance of pain (possible rupture). Surgery should be applied
for adhesion, purulent infection, or perforation (PMID 30014668).

**Pregnancy:** NOT specifically contraindicated.

---

### EX-LE8 — Nội Hoài Tiêm (内踝尖, Nèihuái Jiān)

**Evidence tier: C (Minimum Viable Entry)**

"Medial Malleolus Tip" (内 = inner/medial + 踝 = ankle/malleolus +
尖 = tip/apex) — located at the apex of the medial malleolus. Paired
with EX-LE9 Waihuaijian (lateral malleolus tip). Primarily a
MOXIBUSTION point — the bony prominence has minimal soft tissue for
needling. Traditional indications focus on toothache and sore throat —
distal treatment from the lower extremity.

#### Vị trí giải phẫu

WHO standard location (WPRO 2008): at the apex (most prominent point)
of the medial malleolus of the tibia.

Spatial relationships:
- **KI3 (Taixi):** posterior to the medial malleolus, between malleolus
  and Achilles tendon — ~2 cm posterior to Neihuaijian.
- **KI6 (Zhaohai):** 1 cun below the medial malleolus — directly
  inferior.
- **SP5 (Shangqiu):** anterior-inferior to the medial malleolus.
- **EX-LE9 (Waihuaijian):** lateral malleolus tip — the paired point.

Needle depth: 0.1-0.3 cun. Bony prominence with minimal soft tissue —
MOXIBUSTION is the primary technique (3-7 cones).

#### Cấu trúc mô học tại điểm

Nerve supply:
- **Saphenous nerve (L3-L4):** terminal branches over the medial
  malleolus. A cutaneous branch of the femoral nerve — the longest
  purely sensory nerve in the body.
- Periosteal innervation provides nociceptive input at this bony
  prominence.
- Spinal segments: L3-L4.
→ Cross-ref: A01 S2

Vascular: medial malleolar arterial network (anterior and posterior
tibial arteries). Great saphenous vein passes anterior to the malleolus.

#### Phân loại huyệt

Extra point (经外奇穴): EX-LE8. Not on any meridian.
No special designations.

#### Cross-tradition mapping

TCM acupoint: EX-LE8 Neihuaijian. Functions: clears Heat, benefits
the throat, relieves toothache. ✓
Trigger point: no established TrP at bony prominence. ✗
Ashi: not a consistent tender point. ✗
Marma: **Gulpha Marma** at the ankle joint — includes the malleolar
region. 🟡

**5-8:** No correspondence (Head zone ✗, Anatomy Trains ✗ bony
prominence, Neurogenic ✗, Cupping ✗).

**Convergence score: 2/8** (TCM ✓, Marma 🟡). LOW.

#### Chỉ định truyền thống

- Primary: toothache (牙痛), sore throat (咽喉痛)
- Secondary: tonsillitis, ankle pain
- Primarily treated with MOXIBUSTION (3-7 cones, direct or small cone
  on malleolar tip)
- Classical rationale: opposite-end treatment principle — lower
  extremity distal point for upper body conditions

#### Bằng chứng hiện đại

No systematic review or RCT specific to EX-LE8.

#### An toàn

**Low risk.** Bony prominence with minimal soft tissue — periosteal
pain may be significant with needling. Moxibustion preferred.

**Great saphenous vein:** passes anterior to the medial malleolus —
visible and palpable, avoid direct needling.

**Pregnancy:** NOT specifically contraindicated.

---

### EX-LE9 — Ngoại Hoài Tiêm (外踝尖, Wàihuái Jiān)

**Evidence tier: C (Minimum Viable Entry)**

"Lateral Malleolus Tip" (外 = outer/lateral + 踝 = ankle/malleolus +
尖 = tip/apex) — located at the apex of the lateral malleolus of the
fibula. Paired with EX-LE8 Neihuaijian (medial malleolus tip). Like
its medial counterpart, primarily a MOXIBUSTION point for toothache
and ankle disorders.

#### Vị trí giải phẫu

WHO standard location (WPRO 2008): at the apex (most prominent point)
of the lateral malleolus of the fibula.

Spatial relationships:
- **BL60 (Kunlun):** posterior to the lateral malleolus, between
  malleolus and Achilles tendon — analogous to KI3's position relative
  to the medial malleolus.
- **GB40 (Qiuxu):** anterior-inferior to the lateral malleolus.
- **BL62 (Shenmai):** directly below the lateral malleolus.
- **EX-LE8 (Neihuaijian):** medial malleolus tip — the paired point.

Needle depth: 0.1-0.3 cun. Bony prominence — MOXIBUSTION primary
(3-7 cones).

#### Cấu trúc mô học tại điểm

Nerve supply:
- **Sural nerve (S1-S2):** formed by junction of medial sural cutaneous
  nerve (tibial branch) and lateral sural cutaneous nerve (common
  peroneal branch). Terminal branches distribute over the lateral
  malleolus and lateral foot.
- Periosteal innervation of the fibular malleolus.
- Spinal segments: S1-S2.
→ Cross-ref: A01 S2

Vascular: lateral malleolar arterial network (peroneal artery, anterior
lateral malleolar artery from anterior tibial). Small saphenous vein
passes posterior to the malleolus.

#### Phân loại huyệt

Extra point (经外奇穴): EX-LE9. Not on any meridian.
No special designations.

#### Cross-tradition mapping

TCM acupoint: EX-LE9 Waihuaijian. Functions: clears Heat, relieves
toothache, strengthens the ankle. ✓
Trigger point: no TrP at bony prominence. ✗
Ashi: lateral malleolus tender in ankle inversion sprains —
pathological, not consistent Ashi. ✗
Marma: **Gulpha Marma** — ankle joint area, adjacent. 🟡

**5-8:** No correspondence (Head zone ✗, Anatomy Trains ✗, Neurogenic
✗, Cupping ✗).

**Convergence score: 2/8** (TCM ✓, Marma 🟡). LOW. Matches EX-LE8 —
the paired malleolar points share identical convergence profile.

#### Chỉ định truyền thống

- Primary: toothache (牙痛), lateral ankle pain
- Secondary: foot drop, beriberi (脚气), calcaneal pain
- Primarily treated with MOXIBUSTION (3-7 cones)

#### Bằng chứng hiện đại

No systematic review or RCT specific to EX-LE9.

#### An toàn

**Low risk.** Same considerations as EX-LE8: bony prominence, minimal
soft tissue, periosteal sensitivity. Moxibustion preferred.

**Peroneal tendons:** peroneus longus and brevis tendons pass posterior
to the lateral malleolus in the peroneal groove. Overly posterior
needle placement could contact these structures.

**Pregnancy:** NOT specifically contraindicated.

---

### EX-LE10 — Bát Phong (八风, Bāfēng)

**Evidence tier: C (Minimum Viable Entry)**

"Eight Winds" (八 = eight + 风 = wind) — 8 points (4 per foot) at the
proximal web margins between the toes, on the dorsum of the foot. The
FOOT ANALOG of EX-UE9 Baxie (八邪, "Eight Pathogens," hand web spaces).
While Baxie uses 邪 (pathogen/evil) and Bafeng uses 风 (wind), both
describe the same classical concept: pathogenic factors entering through
the interstices between digits. Extra points recognize hand-foot
anatomical homology more explicitly than the meridian system.

#### Vị trí giải phẫu

WHO standard location (WPRO 2008): on the dorsum of both feet, at the
junction of the web between each pair of toes, proximal to the web
margins. 4 points per foot, 8 total.

Individual point positions:
- **Point 1:** 1st web space (great toe–2nd toe)
- **Point 2:** 2nd web space (2nd–3rd toe)
- **Point 3:** 3rd web space (3rd–4th toe)
- **Point 4:** 4th web space (4th–5th toe)

Spatial relationships:
- **LR3 (Taichong):** in the 1st intermetatarsal space, ~2 cun
  proximal — at the junction of 1st and 2nd metatarsals. Bafeng Point
  1 is at the web fold, ~2-3 cm distal to LR3.
- **ST44 (Neiting):** at the 2nd web space margin — nearly IDENTICAL
  to Bafeng Point 2. ST44 (Ying-Spring/Water of Stomach) occupies
  essentially the same location as Bafeng Point 2 — a meridian-extra
  point overlap where two systems independently identify the same site.
- **EX-UE9 (Baxie):** hand web space equivalent.

Needle depth: 0.5-0.8 cun (12-20 mm) oblique, directed proximally
between the metatarsal shafts.

#### Cấu trúc mô học tại điểm

Nerve supply varies by web space:
- **Point 1 (1st web space):** deep peroneal nerve (L4-L5) — the ONLY
  toe web space innervated by the deep peroneal nerve. Terminal sensory
  branch.
- **Points 2-4 (2nd-4th web spaces):** dorsal digital nerves from the
  superficial peroneal nerve (L4-S1).
- Point 4 may receive contribution from the sural nerve (S1-S2).
→ Cross-ref: A01 S2

Spinal segments: L4-L5 (deep peroneal, Point 1), L4-S1 (superficial
peroneal, Points 2-4).

**Hand-foot nerve comparison (Baxie vs Bafeng):**
- Baxie (hand): radial nerve (C6-C7) → ulnar nerve (C8-T1) gradient
- Bafeng (foot): deep peroneal (L4-L5, 1st only) → superficial
  peroneal (L4-S1, 2nd-4th) gradient
- The hand has GREATER neural diversity (2 major nerve territories)
  than the foot (predominantly superficial peroneal)

Vascular: dorsal metatarsal arteries (from dorsalis pedis). Dorsal
digital arteries at each web space.

#### Phân loại huyệt

Extra point (经外奇穴): EX-LE10. Not on any meridian.
Multi-point group: 4 per foot, 8 total bilaterally.
No special designations.

#### Cross-tradition mapping

TCM acupoint: EX-LE10 Bafeng. Functions: clears Heat, resolves
Dampness, activates collaterals (清热利湿，通络). ✓
Trigger point: dorsal interosseous muscles of the foot have TrPs in
web space territories — less well documented than hand interossei. 🟡
Ashi: toe web spaces may be tender in metatarsalgia, Morton's neuroma
(especially 3rd web space). 🟡

**4-8:** No correspondence (Marma ✗, Head zone ✗, Anatomy Trains ✗,
Neurogenic ✗, Cupping ✗).

**Convergence score: 3/8** (TCM ✓, Trigger point 🟡, Ashi 🟡). LOW.
Lower than hand analog Baxie (4/8) — reflects the foot web spaces'
less documented correspondences.

#### Chỉ định truyền thống

- Primary: foot and toe pain, swelling, numbness
- Secondary: beriberi (脚气), headache (distal treatment), toothache
- Historical: Wind (风) disorders affecting the feet — Wind-Damp Bi
  (风湿痹) of the toes and forefoot

#### Bằng chứng hiện đại

No systematic review or RCT specific to EX-LE10 Bafeng as a distinct
intervention. Bafeng points are occasionally included in diabetic
peripheral neuropathy protocols but not tested in isolation.

**Morton's neuroma note:** the 3rd web space (Point 3) is the most
common site of Morton's neuroma (3rd common digital nerve entrapment
between metatarsal heads). No acupuncture study targets this.

#### An toàn

**Low risk.** Thin tissue between metatarsals, adequate cushion from
dorsal interosseous muscles.

**Diabetic foot caution:** patients with diabetic peripheral neuropathy
may have reduced sensation and impaired wound healing — additional care
with needling at Bafeng.

**Pregnancy:** NOT specifically contraindicated.

---

### EX-LE11 — Độc Âm (独阴, Dúyīn)

**Evidence tier: C (Minimum Viable Entry)**

"Solitary Yin" (独 = alone/solitary + 阴 = Yin) — located on the
PLANTAR surface of the 2nd toe, at the distal interphalangeal joint
crease. One of very few acupoints on the plantar aspect of a toe rather
than the dorsum. The name suggests an isolated Yin point in otherwise
Yang territory (dorsal foot = predominantly Yang channels ST, GB, BL,
LR). Traditional indications focus on gynecological conditions — unusual
for a toe point with no segmental pathway to the uterus (L4-L5 vs
uterine T10-L1).

#### Vị trí giải phẫu

WHO standard location (WPRO 2008): on the plantar surface of the 2nd
toe, at the center of the distal interphalangeal (DIP) joint crease.

Spatial relationships:
- **ST44 (Neiting):** 2nd web space, DORSAL — Duyin is on the PLANTAR
  surface of the same toe.
- **ST45 (Lidui):** lateral nail corner of 2nd toe — at the tip. Duyin
  is more proximal (DIP crease) and plantar.
- **EX-LE12 (Qiduan):** toe tip center — distal end of same toe.
- The 2nd toe is the ONLY toe with acupoints on BOTH dorsal (ST44,
  ST45) and plantar (Duyin) surfaces.

Needle depth: 0.1-0.2 cun perpendicular. Plantar toe skin is thick
but the underlying phalanx is immediately beneath.

#### Cấu trúc mô học tại điểm

Nerve supply:
- **Proper plantar digital nerve of the 2nd toe:** from the 1st common
  digital nerve of the medial plantar nerve (L4-L5, branch of tibial
  nerve).
- Spinal segments: L4-L5 (medial plantar territory).
→ Cross-ref: A01 S2

Vascular: proper plantar digital arteries of the 2nd toe (from lateral
plantar artery via posterior tibial).

#### Phân loại huyệt

Extra point (经外奇穴): EX-LE11. Not on any meridian.
No special designations.

#### Cross-tradition mapping

TCM acupoint: EX-LE11 Duyin. Functions: regulates menstruation,
descends rebellious Qi, calms the fetus (调经，降逆气，安胎). ✓

**2-8:** No correspondence (Trigger point ✗ plantar toe, Ashi ✗,
Marma ✗, Head zone ✗, Anatomy Trains ✗, Neurogenic ✗, Cupping ✗).

**Convergence score: 1/8** (TCM ✓ only). LOW. The plantar toe location
and gynecological indications are TCM-unique — no independent
cross-tradition validation.

#### Chỉ định truyền thống

- Primary: irregular menstruation (月经不调), dysmenorrhea (痛经)
- Secondary: chest pain (心痛), hernia (疝气), retained placenta
  (胞衣不下)
- The gynecological-cardiac combination echoes the classical association
  between the Chong Mai (Thoroughfare Vessel) and lower extremity Yin
  territory, although Duyin has no formal vessel affiliation.

#### Bằng chứng hiện đại

No systematic review or RCT specific to EX-LE11. The mechanism by which
a plantar 2nd toe point (L4-L5) could influence menstrual function is
unexplained by segmental pathways — the uterus receives visceral
innervation from T10-L1, with no L4-L5 connection.

#### An toàn

**Low risk.** Superficial on plantar phalanx. Thick plantar skin may
resist needle insertion — use firm, quick technique.

**Pregnancy:** EXERCISE CAUTION. Classical sources list this point for
"retained placenta" (胞衣不下) and "calming the fetus" — suggesting
potential uterine effects. While no modern evidence supports this, the
traditional pregnancy association warrants prudence.

---

### EX-LE12 — Khí Đoan (气端, Qìduān)

**Evidence tier: C (Minimum Viable Entry)**

"Qi End" (气 = Qi/vital energy + 端 = end/tip) — 10 points at the
tips of all toes, the FOOT ANALOG of EX-UE11 Shixuan (十宣, "Ten
Diffusions," 10 fingertip points). The naming contrast is instructive:
Shixuan 宣 = "to diffuse/proclaim" (active dispersal) vs Qiduan
端 = "end/tip" (terminal point) — the fingers DIFFUSE Qi while the
toes mark where Qi TERMINATES. Both extend the Jing-Well concept
(井穴, "where Qi emerges") from meridian-specific nail corners to
digit-tip centers across ALL digits. The toe tips have significantly
lower sensory receptor density than the fingertips (~6-9× fewer
Meissner corpuscles), predicting weaker nociceptive arousal compared
to Shixuan.

#### Vị trí giải phẫu

WHO standard location (WPRO 2008): on the tips of all 10 toes, at the
center of each toe tip, approximately 0.1 cun (~2 mm) from the free
edge of the toenail. 10 points total (5 per foot).

**Distinction from Jing-Well points (foot):**

| Feature | Jing-Well (6 foot points) | Qiduan (10 points) |
|---------|--------------------------|---------------------|
| Position | Nail CORNER (medial or lateral) | Toe tip CENTER |
| Identity | Meridian-specific (SP1, LR1, ST45, GB44, BL67, KI1*) | Extra point group |
| Toes | Selected toes per meridian | ALL 10 toes |
| Classification | Five Shu: 1st Shu | Extra point, no Five Shu |

*KI1 (Yongquan) is on the plantar sole, not the nail corner — already
displaced from the standard Jing-Well pattern.

Spatial relationships:
- Each toe tip has BOTH a Jing-Well point (nail corner) AND a Qiduan
  point (tip center) — distinct locations ~3-5 mm apart
- **EX-UE11 (Shixuan):** fingertip equivalent
- **EX-LE11 (Duyin):** plantar 2nd toe DIP crease — more proximal

Depth: superficial pricking, 1-2 mm for bloodletting.

#### Cấu trúc mô học tại điểm

Nerve supply (all 10 toe tips):
- **Great toe, 2nd-3rd toes (medial):** proper plantar digital nerves
  from medial plantar nerve (L4-L5, tibial branch)
- **3rd toe (lateral), 4th-5th toes:** proper plantar digital nerves
  from lateral plantar nerve (S1-S2, tibial branch)
- Dorsal contribution from deep peroneal (1st web area) and superficial
  peroneal nerve branches
→ Cross-ref: A01 S2

Spinal segments: L4-L5 (medial plantar, toes 1-3), S1-S2 (lateral
plantar, toes 4-5).

Receptor density comparison with fingertips (Shixuan):
- Meissner corpuscles: ~15-25/cm² (toe pads) vs ~140/cm² (fingertips)
  — roughly 6-9× lower
- Two-point discrimination: ~10 mm at great toe (vs ~2 mm at fingertips)
- Nociceptive density: lower but still substantial
- Predicts LESS INTENSE nociceptive arousal than Shixuan — consistent
  with Shixuan's stronger emergency-revival association

Vascular: proper plantar digital arteries terminate in capillary
networks at each toe tip. Arteriovenous anastomoses present but less
developed than in fingertips.

#### Phân loại huyệt

Extra point (经外奇穴): EX-LE12. Not on any meridian.
Multi-point group: 5 per foot, 10 total bilaterally.
No special designations.

#### Cross-tradition mapping

TCM acupoint: EX-LE12 Qiduan. Functions: clears Heat, revives
consciousness, resolves numbness (清热，开窍，止麻木). ✓

**2-8:** No correspondence (Trigger point ✗ digit tips, Ashi ✗,
Marma ✗, Head zone ✗, Anatomy Trains ✗, Neurogenic ✗, Cupping ✗).

**Convergence score: 1/8** (TCM ✓ only). LOW. The Shixuan-Qiduan pair
is TCM-unique — no independent cross-tradition validation for digit-tip
emergency points.

#### Chỉ định truyền thống

- Primary: numbness and pain of the toes, foot pain
- Secondary: emergency revival (loss of consciousness), febrile seizures
- Pricking/bloodletting technique for emergency — same principle as
  Shixuan but with weaker nociceptive arousal due to lower receptor
  density

#### Bằng chứng hiện đại

No systematic review or RCT specific to EX-LE12. The lower receptor
density at toe tips compared to fingertips suggests weaker nociceptive
arousal than Shixuan — potentially explaining why Shixuan is preferred
for emergency bloodletting (stroke SR/MA 17 RCTs, NIHSS −2.08) while
Qiduan has no comparable evidence.

#### An toàn

**Low risk.** Superficial pricking at toe tips. Standard bloodletting
hygiene applies.

**Diabetic foot caution:** patients with diabetic neuropathy and
peripheral vascular disease are at increased risk of poor wound
healing, infection, and ischemic complications from any foot puncture.
Qiduan pricking should be used with extreme caution in diabetic
patients.

**Pregnancy:** NOT specifically contraindicated.

**ALL 5 REGIONS COMPLETE: EX-HN 15/15, EX-B 8/8, EX-CA 1/1, EX-UE
11/11, EX-LE 12/12.** Total entries in AP-15: 47/48 (including Phase 1
Yintang). No new ⚡ framework notes (running total: 41). Key findings:
(1) both diagnostic extra points (Dannangxue, Lanweixue) are MODERN
discoveries with no classical precedent — the first modern acupoints in
the Atlas; (2) both exhibit SEGMENTAL MISMATCH: organ viscerotome has no
overlap with point somatotome, challenging simple viscero-somatic reflex
explanations; (3) the only formal diagnostic accuracy testing
(Lanweixue, 3 studies) shows POOR performance (sensitivity 22-64%),
substantially inferior to McBurney's sign; (4) hand-foot analog pairs
(Baxie-Bafeng, Shixuan-Qiduan) consistently show the hand member having
higher convergence, more evidence, and greater receptor density; (5) the
malleolus pair (LE8-LE9) = simplest entries in AP-15, both exclusively
moxa points with identical convergence (2/8).*
